\theta_{k}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}\theta_{j}=\delta_{kj}
. The group sparsity assumption is enforced by ensuring that only
k_{1}=20
elements of each canonical vector (the same elements across all vectors) are non-zero. Additionally, the variables
X_{j}
corresponding to the non-zero components of
\theta_{j}
have marginal covariance matrix
\Sigma_{X_{S}}=\text{diag}(\underbrace{2,\ldots 2}_{10},\underbrace{1,\ldots 1}_{10})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k_{1}\times k_{1}}
. The covariance
\Sigma_{X}
is then defined as

\Sigma_{X}=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{X_{S}}\quad 0\\
0\quad I_{p-k_{1}}\end{pmatrix}.

(S2)

The covariance
\Sigma_{Y}
is set to be diagonal with diagonal values being
3,2,1
. The true canonical correlations are chosen to be
\gamma_{1}=.95
and
\gamma_{2}=.6
.

We let the mean curve
\mu
at each
t\in\mathcal{T}
be a SPD matrix
\mu(t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times m}
. We set
\mu(0)
by randomly generating its eigenvectors and setting the associated eigenvalues equal to
\left(1,2,3\right)
. The mean
\mu(t)
at the other time-points
t\in\mathcal{T}
is generated by applying a time-variant rotation to the eigenvectors of
\mu(0)
. We choose each principal component
\phi_{j}
to take the form
\phi_{j}(t)=E(t)P(t)
, for
j=1,\ldots d
, where
E
is chosen at random from a set of orthogonal basis vectors for
L^{2}(T\mu)
, and
P
is chosen at random from a basis of orthogonal polynomials on
[-1,1]
. This ensures that
\{\phi_{j}\}
are orthogonal to one another as elements of
L^{2}(T\mu)
.

In our experiments, we generate
N
i.i.d. pairs
(Y_{i},X_{i})
from the multivariate CCA model in equation (
S1
), for different choices of
N
. Next, we generate
y_{i}
via
\operatorname{Exp}_{\mu}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}Y_{ij}\phi_{j}\right)
and evaluate it at
L=50
locations
t_{l}\in[-1,1]
, yielding
y_{i}(t_{l})
for
i=1,\ldots N
and
l=1,\ldots L
. The observations

\left(\{y_{i}(t_{l})\}_{l},X_{i}\right)_{i},\qquad i=1,\ldots,N,

are used to estimate the canonical vectors and functions and compare different approaches.

A.2
Metrics

We use the following metrics to compare the estimated accuracy of the models considered.

1.

Normalized Euclidean error for the canonical vector

\left\|\theta_{1}/\left\|\theta_{1}\right\|_{2}-\hat{\theta}_{1}/\left\|\hat{\theta}_{1}\right\|_{2}\right\|_{2}

This is a natural metric for evaluating the estimation accuracy.

2.

F1-score for the canonical vector

2\cdot\frac{P\cdot R}{P+R},

where
P=\frac{TP}{TP+FP}
is the precision,
R=\frac{TP}{TP+FN}
is the recall,
TP
is the number of true positives,
FP
the number of false positives,
FN
the number of false negatives.

3.

L^{2}
Parallel transport error for the canonical function

\lVert\Gamma_{\hat{\mu},\mu}\hat{\psi}_{1}-\psi_{1}\lVert_{\mu}

This metric allows us to use the
L^{2}(T\mu)
norm to compare the estimates to the true population analog, by parallel transporting
\hat{\psi}_{1}\in L^{2}(T\hat{\mu})
and defining
\Gamma_{\hat{\mu},\mu}\hat{\psi}_{1}\in L^{2}(T\mu)
.

4.

Tangent Correlation

Using a large test set
\{\tilde{y}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i}\}
, generated from the same distribution as the training data, we compute the sample correlation as follows:

\operatorname{Corr}\left(\left(\llangle\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}\tilde{y}_{i},\Gamma_{\hat{\mu},\mu}\hat{\psi}_{1}\rrangle_{\mu}\right)_{i},\left(\tilde{x}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{1}\right)_{i}\right).

We refer to this metric as the ‘Tangent’ correlation, as it respects the manifold structure of the data.

5.

Euclidean Correlation

Using a large test set
\{\tilde{y}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i}\}
, generated from the same distribution as the training data, we compute the sample correlation as follows:

\operatorname{Corr}\left(\left(\operatorname{vec}\left(\tilde{y}_{i}\right)^{\top}\operatorname{vec}\left(\hat{\psi}_{1}\right)\right)_{i},\left(\tilde{x}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{1}\right)_{i}\right).

We refer to this metric as the ‘Euclidean’ correlation, as it ignores the manifold structure of the data.

A.3
Approaches for comparison

We compare 4 different approaches, detailed below.

1.

Proposed approach.
We apply Algorithm
1
without modifications. We use cross-validation to choose the regularization parameter
\lambda
in the group-lasso regression step as implemented by the
glmnet
package
(
Friedman et al., 2010
)
.

2.

Sparse PCA-based approach:
Intrinsic RFPCA + sparse PCA + classical CCA. We use Intrinsic RFPCA, as in our approach, to reduce the dimensionality of the functional data. We use sparse PCA, using the
elasticnet
R package
(
Zou et al., 2006
)
, to reduce the dimensionality of the multivariate data. Then, we use the estimated PCA scores as input for classical multivariate CCA. We provide sparse PCA with the exact number of principal components that are correlated with the functional data, i.e.,
k_{1}=20
, and restrict the number of non-zero principal loadings per principal component to be
2
.

3.

Sparse CCA-based approach:
Intrinsic RFPCA + sparse CCA. We again use Intrinsic RFPCA to reduce the dimension of the functional data. Next, we use the Penalized Matrix Analysis (PMA) approach to sparse CCA proposed in
Witten et al. (2009)
to compute canonical pairs between the PC scores from Intrinsic RFPCA and the high-dimensional data. The PMA approach to sparse CCA assumes that the covariance matrices of the data are the identity matrices, giving it a slight disadvantage. We choose the amount of penalization for
\theta_{1}
using the suggested permutation-type approach
(
Witten et al., 2009
)
, and choose the penalization parameter for
\eta_{1}
to induce virtually no penalization.

4.

Multivariate FPCA-based approach:
Multivariate FPCA + Asymmetric sparse CCA in Algorithm
2
. This approach is analogous to the one proposed, except that the Intrinsic RFPCA step is replaced by multivariate FPCA
(
Happ and Greven, 2018
)
. Therefore, it disregards the SPD manifold structure of the data. Specifically, it transforms each SPD matrix into a vector extracting the lower triangular part of the matrix. Then it applies multivariate FPCA to the resulting vector-valued functions.

We have chosen these alternative approaches in order to dissect specific components of the CCA problem. Specifically, approach 2 isolates the effect of selecting important features and identifying correlated components in two separate stages, and approach 3 isolates the effect of not taking advantage of the group sparsity structure in the canonical vectors and making restrictive assumptions on the covariance of the high-dimensional data. Approach 4 isolates the effect of treating manifold data as if it were Euclidean. Note that Approach 4 is technically solving a different canonical correlation problem than approaches 1-3 as it aims to maximize the Euclidean correlation rather than the tangent space correlation. For this reason, the underlying population canonical vectors and functions differ from those in the proposed model. Therefore, we only use metric E when evaluating the performance of approach 4.

Moreover, depending on the choice of
\Sigma_{X}
, either the PMA sparse CCA approach or the sparse PCA approach is at a disadvantage. Assuming
\Sigma_{X}
to be the identity matrix meets the assumptions of PMA sparse CCA, but renders dimension reduction through sparse PCA less effective. Conversely, choosing
\Sigma_{X}
not to be the identity matrix benefits sparse PCA at the expense of the PMA sparse CCA approach.

A.4
Results and Discussion

In our experiments, we set
p=200
and vary
N
. For each value of
N
, we run 15 trials. We provide the Intrinsic RFPCA model with the true rank, indicating the number of functional principal components associated with the variable
X
, that is,
d=3
.

Figure 4:
(Top left): Performance evaluation using metric A, which measures the normalized Euclidean error in the first high-dimensional canonical vector, on approaches 1-3. (Top right): Performance evaluation using metric C, which is the parallel transport error in the first canonical function, on approaches 1-3. (Bottom left): Performance evaluation using metric B, the F1-score of the first estimated high-dimensional canonical vector compared to the associated population vector, on approaches 1-3. (Bottom right): Performance evaluation using out-of-sample correlations. We use out-of-sample tangent correlation (metric D) for approaches 1-3, and out-of-sample Euclidean correlation (metric E) for approach 4.

In Figure
4
, we present the performance of approaches 1-3 measured using all defined metrics A-E. As previously mentioned, Approach 4 is assessed using only metric E due to its differing underlying model. In the high-dimensional setting, where
N=50
and
p=200
, all four approaches showed similar performance across all metrics. This setting likely identifies the detectability limits of CCA methods. However, when provided with more samples, our approach quickly outperforms the other approaches. Differences in performance were more notable in the estimation of the Euclidean error for the canonical vectors, F1-score, and out-of-sample correlation. This suggests that the most challenging aspect of the setting considered is estimating the canonical vectors as opposed to the canonical functions. This can be explained by the similar modeling strategies adopted for the functional data. Approach 4, while able to find correlated components in the data according to the Euclidean notion of correlation (E), suffered from bias due to treating the functional data as Euclidean.

For approach 2, the differences in performance can be explained by its two-step strategy that involves first selecting the important features and reducing the dimension of the multivariate data, followed by identifying correlated components. Specifically, the sparse PCA step is based solely on the variance structure of
X
, and not on its correlation with the functional data. In our simulation, the variables of
X
correlated with
Y
have the same or smaller variance than those not correlated with
Y
. As a result, sparse PCA, which is unsupervised, struggles to tease them apart.

Appendix B
Canonical Correlation Analysis of Random Elements of Hilbert Spaces

In this section, we provide a more rigorous formalization of the CCA model. We mirror the development of
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
and
Huang and Renaut (2015)
, but provide a less technical presentation by emphasizing the role of the canonical variables rather than the canonical vectors when formulating the general CCA problem. For an introduction to Hilbert space concepts and random elements taking values in Hilbert spaces, we refer to
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
. For an introduction to classical CCA, we refer to
Uurtio et al. (2018)
.

In Section
B.1
, we define the infinite-dimensional version of the CCA problem, and establish the existence of solutions in our asymmetric setting (Theorem
B.1
). In Section
B.2
, we state preliminary definitions and results for the subsequent sections. In Section
B.3
, we state the necessary assumption (Assumption
B.1
) to reduce the infinite-dimensional CCA problem to a finite-dimensional CCA problem (Theorem
B.2
). In Section
B.4
, we study the difference between the canonical variable solutions of the finite- and infinite-dimensional CCA problems (Theorem
B.3
). In Section
B.5
, we prove the results of the section and additionally prove Theorem
1.1
.

B.1
Problem Statement

Let
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
be measurable functions from a probability space
\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}\right)
to separable Hilbert spaces
\mathbb{H}_{1}
and
\mathbb{H}_{2}
, respectively
(See Section 7.2. of
Hsing and Eubank, 2015
)
. Here,
\mathbb{H}_{1}
and
\mathbb{H}_{2}
are arbitrary Hilbert spaces, but throughout the paper they correspond to
\mathbb{H}_{1}=L^{2}(T\mu)
and
\mathbb{H}_{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{p}
, and similarly
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
correspond to
\chi_{1}=\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y
and
\chi_{2}=X
. Hilbert space inner products are denoted by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, with associated norms
\lVert\cdot\lVert
. The specific choice of the norm or inner product will be clear from the context. We assume that
\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\chi_{i}\lVert^{2}\right]<\infty
so that the mean and covariance of
\chi_{i}
are well-defined for
i=1,2
. The mean element of
\chi_{i}
is defined as
h_{i}\equiv\mathbb{E}\left[\chi_{i}\right]\in\mathbb{H}_{i}
, and for simplicity, we assume
h_{i}=0
for
i=1,2
.

A seemingly natural way to formalize the canonical correlation problem for the infinite-dimensional case, which is analogous to the finite-dimensional case, is

\underset{f\in\mathbb{H}_{1},\textrm{ }g\in\mathbb{H}_{2}}{\operatorname{maximize}\ }\textrm{ Corr}^{2}\left(\langle\chi_{1},f\rangle,\langle\chi_{2},g\rangle\right)

(S3)

where
Corr
is the usual correlation defined between two finite-variance real-valued random variables defined on
\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}\right)
. If they exist, the solution
(f,g)
would be the first canonical vector pair.
Equivalently, we can write this problem in terms of the canonical variables
U,V
as

\underset{U\in\mathcal{U},\textrm{ }V\in\mathcal{V}}{\operatorname{maximize}\ }\textrm{ Corr}^{2}\left(U,V\right)

(S4)

where
\mathcal{U}=\left\{\langle\chi_{1},f\rangle:\textrm{ }f\in\mathbb{H}_{1}\right\}
,
\mathcal{V}=\left\{\langle\chi_{2},g\rangle:\textrm{ }g\in\mathbb{H}_{2}\right\}
.
However, the maximum of this problem may not be attained by any
U\in\mathcal{U}
,
V\in\mathcal{V}

(
Cupidon et al., 2008
)
.

It turns out we can amend this by simply taking the closures of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
. Let
\mathbb{L}^{2}\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}\right)
denote the Hilbert space of square-integrable random variables on
\Omega
with inner product
\langle U,V\rangle=\operatorname{Cov}(U,V)
for
U,V\in\mathbb{L}^{2}\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}\right)
. Note that
U
being square-integrable means that
\operatorname{Var}(U)<\infty
. If we replace

\mathcal{U}
,
\mathcal{V}
in the problem above with their closures as subsets of
\mathbb{L}^{2}\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}\right)
, denoted
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
, (for further discussion of
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
, see the discussion following Example 7.6.5 of
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
) then it can be shown that the maximum
will
be attained for some
U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}
,
V\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}
, provided that a certain linear operator is assumed to be compact. In our setting, this compactness condition holds because we use
\mathbb{H}_{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{p}
, a finite-dimensional space. The result can be found in Theorem 10.1.2 of
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
, which we restate in our context here.

The following result establishes the existence of solutions to the general CCA problem in our asymmetric setting where
\textrm{dim}(\mathbb{H}_{1})=\infty
but
\textrm{dim}(\mathbb{H}_{2})=p<\infty
, and that there are at most
p
nontrivial solutions.

Theorem B.1
.

If
\textrm{dim}(\mathbb{H}_{1})=\infty
and
\textrm{dim}(\mathbb{H}_{2})=p<\infty
, then there exists
U_{1}\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}
and
V_{1}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}
which are solutions to

\underset{U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}},\textrm{ }V\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}}{\operatorname{sup}\ }\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(U,V\right),

with
\operatorname{Var}(U_{1})=\operatorname{Var}(V_{1})=1
. For
k=2,\ldots p
, there exists
U_{k}\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}
and
V_{k}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}
, which are solutions to

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}:\operatorname{Cov}\left(U,U_{i}\right)=0,i=1,\ldots,k-1\\
V\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}:\operatorname{Cov}\left(V,V_{i}\right)=0,i=1,\ldots,k-1\end{subarray}}{\operatorname{sup}}\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(U,V\right),

with
\operatorname{Var}(U_{k})=\operatorname{Var}(V_{k})=1
.
Moreover, for all
U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}
and
V\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}
which are uncorrelated with
U_{1},\ldots U_{p}
,
V_{1},\ldots V_{p}
, respectively, the pair
(U,V)
is a trivial solution, that is,

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}:\operatorname{Cov}\left(U,U_{i}\right)=0,i=1,\ldots,k\\
V\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}:\operatorname{Cov}\left(V,V_{i}\right)=0,i=1,\ldots,k\end{subarray}}{\operatorname{sup}}\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(U,V\right)=0.

The pairs
\{(U_{k},V_{k})\}_{k=1}^{p}
are called the canonical variable pairs. We refer to the problem of finding
\{(U_{k},V_{k})\}_{k=1}^{p}
as the population canonical correlation problem.

Remark 1
.

Intuitively, we must cast the problem in terms of the canonical variables
U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}
,
V\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}
rather than canonical vectors
f\in\mathbb{H}_{1}
,
g\in\mathbb{H}_{2}
because
\mathcal{U}=\left\{\langle\chi_{1},f\rangle:\textrm{ }f\in\mathbb{H}_{1}\right\}
and
\mathcal{V}=\left\{\langle\chi_{2},g\rangle:\textrm{ }g\in\mathbb{H}_{2}\right\}
are not large enough for the supremum of the CCA problem to be attained. To emphasize this point, given an optimal
U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}
of the form
U=\lim_{j\to\infty}\langle\chi_{1},f_{j}\rangle
for some sequence
\left(f_{j}\right)_{j}\in\mathbb{H}_{1}
, then
U
can not necessarily be written as an inner product
U=\langle f,\chi_{1}\rangle
, because
\left(f_{j}\right)_{j}
may not even be a Cauchy sequence in
\mathbb{H}_{1}
, and thus not converge in
\mathbb{H}_{1}
. For further intuition on this property of the problem, see Remark
3
.

In the next section, we introduce an assumption that allows us to make the infinite-dimensional CCA problem finite-dimensional, and furthermore formulate the CCA problem in terms of the canonical vectors rather than the canonical variables. From now on, we assume
\textrm{dim}(\mathbb{H}_{2})=p<\infty
as in Theorem
B.1
.

B.2
Background

We begin with preliminary definitions and properties of the random element
\chi_{1}
. In particular, we introduce the covariance operator
\mathscr{K}_{1}
. The eigenvectors of
\mathscr{K}_{1}
, also known as the principal components of
\chi_{1}
, are fundamental to our approach for two reasons: they provide a data-driven subspace for projecting
\chi_{1}
and their properties simplify our proofs.

Given that
\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\chi_{1}\lVert^{2}\right]<\infty
and
\chi_{1}
has mean
0
, the covariance operator of
\chi_{1}
is well-defined as
\mathscr{K}_{1}\equiv\mathbb{E}\left[\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{1}\right]
. Here, the tensor product
f\otimes g:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}
between
f,g\in\mathbb{H}
for a Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
, is defined as
\left(f\otimes g\right)\left(h\right)=\langle f,h\rangle g
for all
h\in\mathbb{H}
.

In the lemma below, we collect the properties of
\chi_{1}
and
\mathscr{K}_{1}
used in what follows. An orthonormal sequence of elements
\left\{e_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
of a Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
such that
\overline{\text{span}\{e_{j}\}}=\mathbb{H}
is referred to as a complete orthonormal system (CONS) for
\mathbb{H}
. For convenience, we state this result supposing that
\mathscr{K}_{1}
has infinitely many eigenvalues, but an analogous result holds if
\mathscr{K}_{1}
has only finitely many eigenvalues.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}\right)
denote the image of
\mathscr{K}_{1}
and
\overline{\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}\right)}\subseteq\mathbb{H}_{1}
denote its closure in
\mathbb{H}_{1}
. Then, the following statements hold.

1.

With probability 1,
\chi_{1}\in\overline{\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}\right)}
, and for any
f\in\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}\right)^{\perp}
,
\langle f,\chi_{1}\rangle=0

2.

\mathscr{K}_{1}
has the eigendecomposition
\mathscr{K}_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\omega_{j}e_{j}\otimes e_{j}
, where
e_{j}\in\mathbb{H}_{1}
for
j=1,\ldots\infty
and
\omega_{1}\geq\omega_{2}\geq\ldots>0
.
\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
forms a CONS of
\overline{\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}\right)}
, and
\omega_{j}\rightarrow 0
as
j\rightarrow\infty
. We refer to
\{(e_{j},\omega_{j})\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
as the eigensystem of
\mathscr{K}_{1}
, with eigenvectors
e_{j}
and eigenvalues
\omega_{j}
. The
e_{j}
are also referred to as principal components of
\chi_{1}
.

3.

With probability
1
,
\chi_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle e_{j}
. We refer to the
\{\left\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\right\rangle\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
as the principal scores, and they are uncorrelated random variables with
\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle\right]=0
and
\operatorname{Var}\left(\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle\right)=\omega_{j}
.

4.

\overline{\mathcal{U}}=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\omega_{j}a_{j}^{2}<\infty\right\}
and
\{\left\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\right\rangle/\omega_{j}^{1/2}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
forms a CONS for
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
.

Remark 2
.

Item 1 elucidates the role of
\overline{\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}\right)}
as the subspace of
\mathbb{H}_{1}
where
\chi_{1}
resides. Item 2 shows that the eigenvectors
\{e_{j}\}
are a CONS for
\overline{\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}\right)}
, which implies Item 3, that the principal scores
\{\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle\}
characterize
\chi_{1}
. Item 4 establishes that the set of potential canonical variables,
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
, is equivalent to the set of linear combinations of the principal scores with finite variance.

Remark 3
.

Item 4 sheds light on why it is necessary to take the closure of
\mathcal{U}
in order to guarantee a solution to the infinite-dimensional CCA problem in Theorem
B.1
, and further on why the canonical vectors are not defined in the infinite-dimensional CCA problem.

From Items 1 and 2 it follows that

\mathcal{U}\equiv\left\{\langle f,\chi_{1}\rangle:f\in\mathbb{H}_{1}\right\}=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle e_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}<\infty\right\}.

(S5)

For an element
\langle f,\chi_{1}\rangle\in\mathcal{U}
defined by
f=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}e_{j}\in\mathbb{H}_{1}
, since
\operatorname{Var}\left(\langle e_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle\right)=\omega_{j}
, we have that
\operatorname{Var}\left(\langle f,\chi_{1}\rangle\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}\omega_{j}
. Since the
\omega_{j}
decay to
0
as
j
approaches infinite, there are sequences
\left\{a_{j}\right\}
that do not satisfy
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}<\infty
but do satisfy
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}\omega_{j}<\infty
, in other words, there are finite variance linear combinations of the
\langle e_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle
that are not contained in
\mathcal{U}
. These are the elements of
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
that are missing in
\mathcal{U}
:

\mathcal{U}=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle e_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}<\infty\right\}\subset\overline{\mathcal{U}}=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle e_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}\omega_{j}<\infty\right\}.

(S6)

If the canonical variable solution to the infinite-dimensional CCA problem (
S14
) belongs to
\mathcal{U}
but not
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
, then it has no corresponding canonical vector.

For an arbitrary CONS
\left\{e_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
for
\overline{\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}\right)}
, the associated scores
\left\{\langle e_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle\right\}
may not be orthogonal in
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
, but as the following result shows, the associated scores still span
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
. This is the property that allows us to not rely on the principal component basis and instead use an arbitrary CONS for
\mathbb{H}_{1}
in Assumption
B.1
.

Lemma B.2
.

For any complete orthonormal system
\left\{e_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
of
\overline{\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}\right)}
,
\overline{\mathcal{U}}=\overline{\operatorname{span}\left\{\langle e_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle\right\}}
. Thus, any element of
U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}
can be written as
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle
where the
a_{j}
are such that
\operatorname{Var}\left(U\right)<\infty
.

B.3
Reduction to a finite-dimensional problem

We can now introduce Assumption
B.1
on the correlation structure between
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
.

Assumption B.1
.

There exists a complete orthonormal system
\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
for
\mathbb{H}_{1}
and a set of indices
I\subset\left\{1,2,\ldots\right\}
, with finite cardinality
\left|I\right|=d
, such that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Corr}\left(V,\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle\right)=0,\qquad\forall V\in\overline{\mathcal{V}},j\in I^{c},

(S7)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Corr}\left(\langle\chi_{1},e_{i}\rangle,\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle\right)=0,\qquad\forall i\in I,\forall j\in I^{c},

(S8)

where
I^{c}
denotes the complement of
I
in
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,\ldots\}
.

Remark 4
.

The complete orthonormal system
\left\{e_{j}\right\}_{j}
is not required to be the principal component basis.
In the case when
\mathbb{H}_{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and
\chi_{2}=X
, equation (
S7
) can be rewritten as

\operatorname{Corr}\left(X_{i},\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle\right)=0,\qquad\forall i=1,\ldots p,j\in I^{c}.

(S9)

Intuitively, this assumption states that all elements
\psi
of
\mathbb{H}_{1}
whose projections
\langle\chi_{1},\psi\rangle
are correlated with
X
belong to a
d
-dimensional subspace.

Remark 5
.

Assumption
B.1
is weaker than the assumption that
\chi_{1}
admits a finite-dimensional representation
\chi_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle e_{j}
, for a set of vectors
\left\{e_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{d}\subset\mathbb{H}_{1}
. To see this, we first note that the elements
\left\{e_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{d}\subset\mathbb{H}_{1}
are orthonormal. Then, we complete
\left\{e_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{d}\subset\mathbb{H}_{1}
to form a CONS
\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
for
\mathbb{H}_{1}
, and take
I=\{1,\ldots d\}
. Given that the elements
\{e_{j}\}
are orthonormal, we have that
\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle=0
for all
j\in I^{c}
, with probability
1
. Hence, conditions (
S7
) and (
S8
) are satisfied.

Making this assumption enables us to reduce the infinite-dimensional CCA problem to a finite-dimensional CCA problem; moreover, it allows us to formulate the CCA problem in terms of the population quantities of interest, the canonical vectors, rather than the canonical variables (Theorem
B.2
). Theorem
B.2
can be viewed as a generalization of Theorem 1 of
Krzyśko and Waszak (2013)
. There, it is assumed that
\chi_{1}
has a finite-dimensional representation, whereas here we make the weaker Assumption
B.1
.

Theorem B.2
.

Reorder the complete orthonormal system
\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
for
\mathbb{H}_{1}
in Assumption
B.1
so that
I=\left\{1,\ldots d\right\}
. Then, under Assumption
B.1
, the solution to the population canonical correlation problem in Theorem
B.1
is found for a
U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{d}=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{d}a_{i}\langle\chi_{1},e_{i}\rangle:a_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}\right\}
.

Moreover, when
\mathbb{H}_{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and
\chi_{2}=X
, the problem is equivalent to the following multivariate (finite-dimensional) canonical correlation problem, where
Y
is the
d
-dimensional random vector such that
Y_{j}=\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle
, the
j
th score associated with
e_{j}
, for
j=1,\ldots d
:

\displaystyle(a_{1},b_{1})

\displaystyle=\underset{a\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},b\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p},\operatorname{Var}(a^{\top}Y)=\operatorname{Var}(b^{\top}X)=1}{\operatorname{arg\,max\,}}\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(a^{\top}Y,b^{\top}X\right),

(S10)

\displaystyle(a_{k},b_{k})

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},b\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p},\operatorname{Var}(a^{\top}Y)=\operatorname{Var}(b^{\top}X)=1\\
\operatorname{Cov}\left(a^{\top}Y,a_{i}^{\top}Y\right)=0,i=1,\ldots,k\\
\operatorname{Cov}\left(b^{\top}X,b_{i}^{\top}X\right)=0,i=1,\ldots,k\end{subarray}}{\operatorname{arg\,max\,}}\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(a^{\top}Y,b^{\top}X\right),\qquad k=2,\ldots min(p,d).

(S11)

We call the pair
\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{kj}e_{j},b_{k}\right)
the
k
th canonical pair, since
a^{\top}Y=\langle\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{kj}e_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle
, and
b^{\top}X=\langle b,\chi_{2}\rangle
, where
a_{kj}
is the
j
th entry of
a_{k}
, for
k=1,\ldots\operatorname{min}(p,d)
.

This result is central to the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

B.4
Error between finite- and infinite-dimensional problems

In this section, we analyze the error between the finite- and infinite-dimensional CCA problems. In particular, we quantify the error between the canonical variables obtained from solving these problems. We continue to write as
(U_{k},V_{k})
the canonical variable solutions to the infinite-dimensional CCA problem in Theorem
B.1
, while we denote as
(U_{k}^{(d)},V_{k}^{(d)})
the canonical variable solutions to the finite-dimensional CCA problem in Theorem
B.2
where we have used a
d
-dimensional Assumption
B.1
. Thus, by definition,
U_{k}^{(d)}=\eta_{k}^{\top}Y
and
V_{k}^{(d)}=\theta_{k}^{\top}X
.

The relationship between these problems is well-understood, and we refer to Chapter 10 of
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
for further background. We now introduce the notation and machinery necessary to write down the error between
U_{k}
and
U^{(d)}_{k}
, and
V_{k}
and
V^{(d)}_{k}
, provided in Theorem
B.3
. This result is used to show Theorem
E.1
.

For the remainder of this section, we suppose that the complete orthonormal system
\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
for
\mathbb{H}_{1}
in Assumption
B.1
are the principal components of
\chi_{1}
(extended from a CONS for
\overline{\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}\right)}
).

B.4.1
Background

The idea central to deriving our bound is that there are spaces
\mathbb{G}_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{H}_{1}
and
\mathbb{G}_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{H}_{2}
that are congruent to
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
, respectively, so that the infinite-dimensional CCA problem in Theorem
B.1
can be written over
\mathbb{G}_{1}
and
\mathbb{G}_{2}
instead of
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
, and subsequently in terms of an operator,
\mathscr{C}_{12}
, between
\mathbb{G}_{1}
and
\mathbb{G}_{2}
. The infinite-dimensional CCA solution is then derived from the singular vector decomposition of
\mathscr{C}_{12}
, while the solution to the finite-dimensional CCA problem is derived from the singular vector decomposition of a principal component-approximation of
\mathscr{C}_{12}
,
\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}
. The error between
\mathscr{C}_{12}
and
\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}
quantifies the error between the canonical variable solutions for the two problems.

Define

\mathbb{G}_{1}\equiv\text{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}^{1/2}\right)=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\omega_{j}^{1/2}a_{j}e_{j}:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}<\infty\right\},

(S12)

where
(\omega_{j},e_{j})
are the eigenvector-eigenvalue pairs of
\mathscr{K}_{1}
. We define a new inner product on
\mathbb{G}_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{H}_{1}
, which is not the one inherited from
\mathbb{H}_{1}
. For
f,g\in\mathbb{G}_{1}
with representations
f=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\omega_{j}^{1/2}a_{j}e_{j}
and
g=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\omega_{j}^{1/2}b_{j}e_{j}
, we define
\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathbb{G}_{1}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}b_{j}
. It is straightforward to verify that
\mathbb{G}_{1}
with this inner product is a Hilbert space with a CONS
\left\{\tilde{e}_{1j}\right\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
where
\tilde{e}_{1j}\equiv\omega_{j}^{1/2}e_{j}
.

We have the following congruency between
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{1}
. We say that two Hilbert spaces,
\mathbb{H}_{1}
and
\mathbb{H}_{2}
with norms
\lVert\cdot\lVert_{1}
and
\lVert\cdot\lVert_{2}
, respectively, are congruent if there exists a bijective function
\pi:\mathbb{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}
such that
\lVert f-g\lVert_{1}=\lVert\pi(f)-\pi(g)\lVert_{2}
for all
f,g\in\mathbb{H}_{1}
.

Lemma B.3
.

\mathbb{G}_{1}
is congruent to
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
, with
\pi:\mathbb{G}_{1}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{U}}
defined as the map which takes
f\in\mathbb{G}_{1}
with representation
f=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\tilde{e}_{1j}
to its image
\pi(f)\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}
:

\pi(f)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\frac{\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle}{\omega_{j}^{1/2}}.

(S13)

The proof of this result follows directly from Theorem 2.4.16 of
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
, recalling that
\left\{{\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle}/{\omega_{j}^{1/2}}\right\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
form a CONS for
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
by Item 4 of Lemma
B.1
.

We define
\mathbb{G}_{2}
and
\pi_{2}:\mathbb{G}_{2}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{V}}
analogously based on the covariance operator for
\chi_{2}
for clarity of our presentation, but note that since
\mathbb{H}_{2}
is assumed to be finite-dimensional,
\mathbb{G}_{2}
is also finite-dimensional, with
\text{dim}\left(\mathbb{G}_{2}\right)=p
. We similarly define a CONS
\left\{\tilde{e}_{2j}\right\}_{j=1}^{p}
for
\mathbb{G}_{2}
.

We can now write the problem over
U,V
, which reads as

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}},\textrm{ }V\in\overline{\mathcal{V}},\\
\operatorname{Var}(U)=\operatorname{Var}(V)=1\end{subarray}}{\operatorname{maximize}\ }\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(U,V\right),

(S14)

in terms of elements of
\mathbb{G}_{1}
and
\mathbb{G}_{2}
instead:

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in\mathbb{G}_{1},\textrm{ }g\in\mathbb{G}_{2},\\
\operatorname{Var}(\pi_{1}(f))=\operatorname{Var}(\pi_{2}(g))=1\end{subarray}}{\operatorname{maximize}\ }\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(\pi_{1}(f),\pi_{2}(g)\right).

(S15)

We define an operator
\mathscr{C}_{12}
between
\mathbb{G}_{2}
and
\mathbb{G}_{1}
such that the following property holds. For any
f\in\mathbb{G}_{1}
,
g\in\mathbb{G}_{2}
, we have

\operatorname{Cov}\left(\pi_{1}(f),\pi_{2}(g)\right)=\langle f,\mathscr{C}_{12}g\rangle_{\mathbb{G}_{1}}.

(S16)

This operator exists, is bounded, and has
\lVert\mathscr{C}_{12}\lVert\leq 1
by Theorem 10.1.1 of
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
. We denote by
\mathscr{C}_{21}:\mathbb{G}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{2}
the adjoint of
\mathscr{C}_{12}
, which satisfies
\operatorname{Cov}\left(\pi_{1}(f),\pi_{2}(g)\right)=\langle\mathscr{C}_{21}f,g\rangle_{\mathbb{G}_{2}}
.

In our setting where
\mathbb{G}_{2}
is finite-dimensional,
\mathscr{C}_{12}
is immediately compact, so that we can solve (
S15
) in terms of the singular vector decomposition of
\mathscr{C}_{12}
. In fact, taking the singular vector decomposition of
\mathscr{C}_{12}
and applying equation (
S16
) comprise the proof of Theorem
B.1
. We note that if
\mathbb{G}_{2}
were infinite-dimensional, we would need to assume compactness of
\mathscr{C}_{12}
.

B.4.2
Equivalence of finite-dimensional canonical variables

Before stating our error bound, we need one more result on an equivalent definition of the canonical variables
U_{k}^{(d)}
and
V_{k}^{(d)}
. We additionally introduce a principal component approximation of
\mathscr{C}_{12}
.

Since we have CONS
\left\{\tilde{e}_{1i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
\left\{\tilde{e}_{2j}\right\}_{j=1}^{p}
for
\mathbb{G}_{1}
and
\mathbb{G}_{2}
, respectively, we can write
\mathscr{C}_{12}
as

\mathscr{C}_{12}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\langle\tilde{e}_{2j},\mathscr{C}_{12}\tilde{e}_{1i}\rangle_{\mathbb{G}_{1}}.

(S17)

We then define its finite-dimensional approximation by the first
d
principal components of
\mathscr{K}_{1}
as

\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\langle\tilde{e}_{2j},\mathscr{C}_{12}\tilde{e}_{1i}\rangle_{\mathbb{G}_{1}}.

(S18)

We define
\mathscr{C}_{21}^{(d)}
in an analogous way.

Lemma B.4
.

Recall
\left(U_{k}^{(d)},V_{k}^{(d)}\right)
, the canonical variables obtained by solving the finite-dimensional CCA problem in Theorem
B.2
obtained by making a
d
-dimensional Assumption
B.1
:

\left(U_{k}^{(d)},V_{k}^{(d)}\right)=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{d},V\in\overline{\mathcal{V}},\\
\operatorname{Var}(U)=\operatorname{Var}(V)=1\end{subarray}}{\operatorname{arg\,max\,}}\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(U,V\right).

(S19)

Let
\left(f_{k},g_{k}\right)
be the
k
th pair of right and left singular vectors of
\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}
, respectively. Let
\left(\tilde{U}_{k}^{(d)},\tilde{V}_{k}^{(d)}\right)
be defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{U}_{k}^{(d)}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\left(f_{k}\right),

(S20)

\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{k}^{(d)}

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\left(g_{k}\right).

(S21)

Then,
U_{k}^{(d)}=\tilde{U}_{k}^{(d)}
and
\tilde{V}_{k}^{(d)}=V_{k}^{(d)}
.

Remark 6
.

In this paper, we have defined
\left(U_{k}^{(d)},V_{k}^{(d)}\right)
directly as the solution to a finite-dimensional CCA problem. In
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
, finite-dimensional canonical variables are studied and defined as
\left(\tilde{U}_{k}^{(d)},\tilde{V}_{k}^{(d)}\right)
. This result shows that these two definitions are equivalent.

B.4.3
Error bound

We can finally state the following result about the error in making a
d
-dimensional Assumption
B.1
. The proof follows directly from Theorem 5.2.2 and the proof of equation (10.19) in Theorem 10.2.3 in
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
, the triangle inequality, and Lemma
B.4
.

Theorem B.3
.

Let
(U_{k},V_{k})
be the infinite-dimensional canonical variables obtained by solving the problem in Theorem
B.1
. Let
\left(U_{k}^{(d)},V_{k}^{(d)}\right)
be the canonical variables obtained by solving the finite-dimensional CCA problem in Theorem
B.2
obtained by making a
d
-dimensional Assumption
B.1
with the principal components of
\chi_{1}
. Then for
k\leq d
, as
d\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\max\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(U_{k}-U_{k}^{(d)}\right)^{2}\right],\mathbb{E}\left[\left(V_{k}-V_{k}^{(d)}\right)^{2}\right]\right\}\lesssim\frac{{\gamma_{1}^{*}}^{2}\lVert\mathscr{C}_{12}-\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}\lVert^{2}}{\min_{j\neq k}\left|{\gamma_{k}^{*}}^{2}-{\gamma_{j}^{*}}^{2}\right|},

(S22)

where
\gamma_{i}^{*}
denote the infinite-dimensional canonical correlations, i.e. the singular values of
\mathscr{C}_{12}
.

This result is used in the proof of Theorem
E.1
.

B.5
Proofs

Proof of Lemma
B.1
:

The first item is part 3 of Theorem 7.2.5 of
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
. The second and third items are Theorem 7.2.6 and Theorem 7.2.7 of
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
, respectively.
For the fourth item, equation (
S5
) gives

\mathcal{U}\equiv\left\{\langle f,\chi_{1}\rangle:f\in\mathbb{H}_{1}\right\}=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle e_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}<\infty\right\}.

(S23)

From this, it is clear that
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\omega_{j}a_{j}^{2}<\infty\right\}
, because
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}
implies
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\omega_{j}a_{j}^{2}
. We also have that

\overline{\text{span}\left(\left\{\right\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle\}_{j=1}^{\infty}\right)}=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\omega_{j}a_{j}^{2}<\infty\right\},

(S24)

from the definition of the norm in
\mathbb{L}^{2}\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}\right)
and because we can calculate the norm squared as
\operatorname{Var}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\omega_{j}a_{j}^{2}
by the continuity of the inner product. In particular, this set is closed, so
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\omega_{j}a_{j}^{2}<\infty\right\}
implies
\overline{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\omega_{j}a_{j}^{2}<\infty\right\}
. To show the reverse inclusion, it suffices to show that
{\text{span}\left(\left\{\right\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle\}_{j=1}^{\infty}\right)}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, which is clear from equation (
S5
).

That
\{\left\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\right\rangle/\omega_{j}^{1/2}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
forms a CONS for
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
follows from
\overline{\mathcal{U}}=\overline{\text{span}\left(\left\{\right\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle\}_{j=1}^{\infty}\right)}
as was just shown, and normalizing the already orthogonal
\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle
to have norm
1
. ∎

Proof of Lemma
B.2
:

We begin by employing the fourth item of Lemma
B.1
, which states that
\{\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle/\omega_{j}^{1/2}\}
is a CONS for
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
, where the
e_{j}
are the eigenvectors of
\mathscr{K}_{1}
, and
\omega_{j}
are the corresponding eigenvalues. Therefore, given an arbitrary CONS for
\overline{\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}\right)}
,
\{f_{j}\}_{j=1,\ldots\infty}
, to complete the proof it suffices to show that
\overline{\text{span}\left\{\langle f_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle\right\}}=\overline{\text{span}\left\{\langle e_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle\right\}}
.

To show the
\subseteq
direction, it suffices to show that
\langle f_{k},\chi_{1}\rangle\in\overline{\text{span}\left\{\langle e_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle\right\}}
, for every
k
, by the definitions of closure and span of a set of vectors. Since the functions
\{e_{j}\}
form a CONS for
\overline{\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathscr{K}_{1}\right)}
, there exists a sequence
\left(a_{j}\right)_{j=1}^{\infty}
of scalars such that
f_{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}e_{j}
. Therefore,
\langle f_{k},\chi_{1}\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle
by continuity of the inner product on
\mathbb{H}_{1}
, and we have
\langle f_{k},\chi_{1}\rangle\in\overline{\text{span}\left\{\langle e_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle\right\}}
.

To show the
\supseteq
direction, we must show that
\langle e_{k},\chi_{1}\rangle\in\overline{\text{span}\left\{\langle f_{j},\chi_{1}\rangle\right\}}
for every
k
, which follows by similar arguments. ∎

Proof of Theorem
B.2
:

We prove the statement for the first canonical pair; the proof for the remaining canonical pairs follows from a similar argument. Let
(U,V)
be the first canonical pair of the population canonical correlation problem in Theorem
B.1
. We consider the CONS
\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
for
\mathbb{H}_{1}
from Assumption
B.1
, and reorder its elements so that
I=\left\{1,\ldots d\right\}
. By Lemma
B.2
, we write
U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}
as
U=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle
. We will show that under Assumption
B.1
, we can find a
Q=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}q_{j}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}
, with
q_{j}=0
for
j>d
, that attains the same maximum value as
U
. Thus we will have
Q\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{d}\equiv\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{d}a_{i}\langle\chi_{1},e_{i}\rangle:a_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}\right\}
, completing the proof of the first statement of the Theorem. For random variables with variance
1
, such as
U
and
V
, we have that
\operatorname{Cov}(U,V)=\operatorname{Corr}(U,V)
. We use these interchangeably throughout the proof.

If the optimum value of
\operatorname{Corr}^{2}(U,V)
is
0
, then we can select any
Q
with
q_{j}=0
for
j>d
and
\operatorname{Var}(Q)=1
. Therefore, from now on, we focus on the case where
\operatorname{Corr}^{2}(U,V)\neq 0
.
Let

U=\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{j}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle+\sum_{j=d+1}^{\infty}a_{j}\langle\chi_{1},e_{j}\rangle\equiv W+Z.

(S25)

Then, from continuity of the inner product and Assumption
B.1
, it follows that
\operatorname{Cov}(Z,V)=0
and
\operatorname{Cov}(W,Z)=0
, from conditions (
S7
) and (
S8
) respectively.
Before constructing
Q
, we note that the variance of
W
must be less than or equal to
1
. To see this, we use

1=\operatorname{Var}(U)=\operatorname{Var}(W+Z)=\operatorname{Var}(W)+2\operatorname{Cov}(W,Z)+\operatorname{Var}(Z)=\operatorname{Var}(W)+\operatorname{Var}(Z),

(S26)

since
\operatorname{Cov}(W,Z)=0
. Then,
\operatorname{Var}(W)\leq 1
since both
\operatorname{Var}(W)
and
\operatorname{Var}(Z)
are positive and sum to
1
.

Now, we construct a canonical variable
Q
with the desired property. The optimal value of the CCA population problem in Theorem
B.1
under assumption
B.1
is

\displaystyle\operatorname{Corr}^{2}(U,V)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Cov}^{2}(W+Z,V)

(S27)

\displaystyle=\left(\operatorname{Cov}(W,V)+\operatorname{Cov}(Z,V)\right)^{2}

(S28)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Cov}^{2}(W,V)

(S29)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Corr}^{2}(W,V)\operatorname{Var}(W).

(S30)

Having established
\operatorname{Var}(W)\leq 1
, there are three cases, either
\operatorname{Var}(W)=0
,
0<\operatorname{Var}(W)<1
, or
\operatorname{Var}(W)=1
. In the case
\operatorname{Var}(W)=1
, we take
Q=W
, and using equation (
S30
), we see that the pair
(W,V)
attains the same maximum correlation as
(U,V)
. This completes the proof as
W
is of the desired form.
Now, we will show that the other two cases,
\operatorname{Var}(W)=0
,
0<\operatorname{Var}(W)<1
, are not possible.
\operatorname{Var}(W)=0
cannot hold since, by equation (
S30
), we would have
\operatorname{Cov}(U,V)=0
, which we have already ruled out. Assume towards a contradiction that
0<\operatorname{Var}(W)<1
, let
c=\frac{1}{\operatorname{Var}(W)^{1/2}}>1
, and define
Q=cW
. Then, we have that
\operatorname{Var}(Q)=c^{2}\operatorname{Var}(W)=1
, and

\operatorname{Corr}^{2}(U,V)=\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(W,V\right)\operatorname{Var}(W)<\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(Q,V\right),

(S31)

by equation (
S30
),
\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(W,V\right)=\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(Q,V\right)
, and
\operatorname{Var}(W)<1
. However, this is a contradiction as it would imply that the pair
(Q,V)
attains a larger value of the objective than
(U,V)
. This completes the proof of the first statement.

Having established the existence of a solution of the stated form for
Q
, that we are able to reformulate the CCA problem in terms of the finite-dimensional vectors
\{a_{k}\}_{k}
rather than
U\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}
follows from the definition of
\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{d}
and the bilinearity of the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
\mathbb{H}_{1}
. In the case that
\mathbb{H}_{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and
\chi_{2}=X
, we are able to reformulate the problem in terms of the finite-dimensional vectors
\{b_{k}\}_{k}
rather than
V\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}
due to the following argument. We have
\mathcal{V}\equiv\{\langle\chi_{2},g\rangle:\textrm{ }g\in\mathbb{H}_{2}\}=\operatorname{span}\left\{\langle\chi_{2},e_{j}\rangle,j=1,\ldots p\right\}
(where the
e_{j}
here are the standard unit vectors for
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
) is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
, which is complete. Thus,
\{\langle\chi_{2},g\rangle:\textrm{ }g\in\mathbb{H}_{2}\}
is complete, so its completion in
\mathbb{L}^{2}\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}\right)
is itself, i.e.
\overline{\mathcal{V}}=\mathcal{V}
. Therefore,
\overline{\mathcal{V}}=\operatorname{span}\left\{\langle\chi_{2},e_{j}\rangle,j=1,\ldots p\right\}=\{g^{\top}X:g\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\}
, i.e. the set of linear combinations of
X_{1},\ldots X_{p}
.

The number of nontrivial canonical variables has changed from
p
in Theorem
B.1
to
\operatorname{min}(p,d)
. This is because, in a finite-dimensional CCA problem concerning random vectors of dimensions
p
and
d
, the smaller of the two dimensions is the upper limit for the number of nontrivial canonical variables
(
Uurtio et al., 2018
)
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.4
:

Using the congruency of
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
with
\mathbb{G}_{1}
and
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
with
\mathbb{G}_{2}
, the optimization problem in (
S19
) can be rewritten as

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in\text{span}\left(\tilde{e}_{11},\ldots\tilde{e}_{1d}\right),g\in\mathbb{G}_{2},\\
\operatorname{Var}\left(\pi_{1}(f)\right)=\operatorname{Var}\left(\pi_{2}(g)\right)=1\end{subarray}}{\operatorname{maximize}\ }\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(\pi_{1}(f),\pi_{2}(g)\right).

(S32)

Using the definition of
\mathscr{C}_{12}
, we have

\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(\pi_{1}(f),\pi_{2}(g)\right)=\langle\mathscr{C}_{21}f,g\rangle_{\mathbb{G}_{2}}^{2}.

(S33)

Because
f\in\text{span}\left(\tilde{e}_{11},\ldots\tilde{e}_{1d}\right)
, we obtain
\langle\mathscr{C}_{21}f,g\rangle_{\mathbb{G}_{2}}=\langle\mathscr{C}_{21}^{(d)}f,g\rangle_{\mathbb{G}_{2}}=\langle f,\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}g\rangle_{\mathbb{G}_{1}}
.
Therefore, the maximum of this problem is determined by the singular vectors of
\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}
, and we see that
{U}_{k}^{(d)}=\pi_{1}\left(f_{k}\right)
and
{V}_{k}^{(d)}=\pi_{2}\left(g_{k}\right)
. ∎

B.6
Proof of Theorem
1.1

Given that Assumption
1.1
is equivalent to Assumption
B.1
, by applying Theorem
B.2
, we readily derive the first part of the theorem. This establishes that there are at most
{d^{(\text{corr})}}
nontrivial canonical variable pairs
(U_{k},V_{k})
. Moreover, each pair
(U_{k},V_{k})
can be written in terms of the canonical directions
U_{k}=\llangle\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y,\psi_{k}\rrangle_{\mu}
and
V_{k}=X^{\top}\theta_{k}
, for some
\psi_{k}\in L^{2}(T\mu)
and
\theta_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
. Additionally,
(\psi_{k},\theta_{k})=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{kj}\phi_{j},b_{k}\right)
, where the pairs
(a_{k},b_{k})
are defined in Theorem
B.2
as the solution to a multivariate CCA problem, and the functions
\{\phi_{j}\}
form the CONS for
L^{2}(T\mu)
, defined in Section
1.3
.

It remains to be shown that the solutions
(a_{k},b_{k})
to the multivariate CCA problem can be characterized by the equations (
4
)-(
7
). We focus on the finite-dimensional optimization problem, in equation (
S10
), that defines the first canonical pair. This is equivalent to

\underset{a_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}a_{1}=1=b_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{Y}b_{1}}{\operatorname{sup}\ }a_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}b_{1}.

Now using the assumption that
\Sigma_{X}
and
\Sigma_{Y}
are invertible, we make a change of variables
\tilde{a}_{1}=\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}a_{1}
,
\tilde{b}_{1}=\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}b_{1}
and obtain the equivalent problem

\underset{\tilde{a}_{1}^{\top}\tilde{a}_{1}=1=\tilde{b}_{1}^{\top}\tilde{b}_{1}}{\operatorname{sup}\ }\tilde{a}_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\tilde{b}_{1}

Let
U\Gamma V^{\top}=\Sigma_{X}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}
be a singular value decomposition of
\Sigma_{X}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}
, where
U\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times{d^{(\text{corr})}}}
,
\Gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{{d^{(\text{corr})}}\times{d^{(\text{corr})}}}
,
V\in{\mathbb{R}}^{{d^{(\text{corr})}}\times{d^{(\text{corr})}}}
,
U^{\top}U=I_{d^{(\text{corr})}}=V^{\top}V
, and where
\Gamma
is a diagonal matrix with the diagonal elements
\gamma_{1},\ldots\gamma_{d^{(\text{corr})}}
, in descending order. Note that
p\geq{d^{(\text{corr})}}
. Then it follows from standard properties of the SVD that the first columns of
U
and
V
, denoted as
u_{1}
and
v_{1}
respectively, are the solutions to the above problem, i.e.
(\tilde{a}_{1},\tilde{b}_{1})=(u_{1},v_{1})
.
Similarly, it can be shown that the
k
th columns of
U
and
V
,
u_{k}
and
v_{k}
respectively, are such that
(\tilde{a}_{k},\tilde{b}_{k})=(u_{k},v_{k})
, and that the optimal correlations are the singular values
\gamma_{1},\ldots\gamma_{d^{(\text{corr})}}
. Undoing the change of variables, it can be seen that the solutions to the original problems in equation (
S10
) are the pairs formed by the
k
th columns of the matrices
\Sigma_{X}^{-1/2}U
and
\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}V
. The associated squared correlations are the diagonal entries of
\Gamma^{2}
.

Now let
B
be the solution to the optimization problem

\underset{B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times{d^{(\text{corr})}}}}{\operatorname{minimize}\ }\textrm{ }\mathbb{E}\left[\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}Y-B^{\top}X\|_{2}^{2}\right].

(S34)

It is straightforward to show that
B=\Sigma_{X}^{-1}\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}
. Therefore, we have

\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}B=U\Gamma V^{\top},

(S35)

B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B=V\Gamma^{2}V^{\top}

(S36)

and

BV\Gamma^{-1}=\Sigma_{X}^{-1/2}U.

(S37)

Identifying
\tilde{H}
,
H
, and
T
in equations (
4
)-(
7
) with
V
,
\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}V
, and
\Sigma_{X}^{-1/2}U
, respectively, completes the proof.

Appendix C
Asymmetric Sparse CCA: Proof of Theorem
3.1

C.1
Notation

For a vector
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
with entries
\{x_{j}\}
we define its infinity norm
\left\|x\right\|_{\infty}=\operatorname{max}_{j}(|x_{j}|)
, its Euclidean norm
\left\|x\right\|_{2}=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{p}x_{j}^{2}}
, and its
\ell_{1}
norm
\left\|x\right\|_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}|x_{j}|
. For a matrix
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times d}
with singular values
\sigma_{1},\ldots\sigma_{d}
, its operator norm is
\left\|A\right\|_{2}=\operatorname{max}_{i}(|\sigma_{i}|)
. To denote the
i
th row of the matrix
A
, we use
A_{i}
, and for the entry in the
i
th row and
j
th column, we use
a_{ij}
. We define the matrix norms
\left\|A\right\|_{F}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{ij}^{2}\right)^{1/2}
,
\left\|A\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\left\|A_{i}\right\|_{2}
, and
\left\|A\right\|_{\operatorname{max}}=\max_{(i,j)}|a_{i,j}|
.

Given the normed spaces
({\mathbb{R}}^{d},\lVert\cdot\lVert_{\alpha})
and
({\mathbb{R}}^{p},\lVert\cdot\lVert_{\beta})
, and a matrix
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times d}
, we define the matrix norm induced by
\lVert\cdot\lVert_{\alpha}
and
\lVert\cdot\lVert_{\beta}
as

\lVert A\lVert_{\alpha,\beta}=\underset{\lVert x\lVert_{\alpha}=1}{\operatorname{sup}\ }\lVert Ax\lVert_{\beta}.

(S38)

For additional properties of the matrix norms used throughout the paper, we refer to Section
F
.
We use the notation
x\lesssim y
for
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}
to indicate that
x\leq Cy
, with
C
some positive absolute constant.

C.2
Sub-Gaussian random vectors

Now we briefly define sub-Gaussian random vectors and state basic properties that we use in the proofs. We refer the reader to
Vershynin (2018)
for a more comprehensive introduction to sub-Gaussian random variables and vectors.

A random variable
X
is sub-Gaussian if, for some constant
C>0
, it satisfies

\mathbb{P}\{|X|\geq t\}\leq 2\exp\left(-t^{2}/C\right)\quad\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

(S39)

The sub-Gaussian norm of
X
is defined as

\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}=\inf\left\{t>0:\mathbb{E}\exp\left(X^{2}/t^{2}\right)\leq 2\right\}.

(S40)

A random vector
X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is called sub-Gaussian if
\langle X,x\rangle
is sub-Gaussian for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
. The sub-Gaussian norm of
X
is defined as

\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}=\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}:\left\|x\right\|_{2}=1}\|\langle X,x\rangle\|_{\psi_{2}}.

(S41)

From its definition, it is clear that
\left\|X_{i}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\left\|X\right\|_{\psi_{2}}
, where
X_{i}
is the
i
th element of
X
. To simplify our analysis, we will also assume that sub-Gaussian vectors
X
satisfy the variance-proxy condition defined below.

Definition C.1
.

A sub-Gaussian random vector
X
satisfies the variance-proxy condition if there exists a constant
K_{X}
such that for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,
\left\|\langle X,x\rangle\right\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq K_{X}\operatorname{Var}\left(\langle X,x\rangle\right)^{1/2}
.

Intuitively, this condition implies that the sub-Gaussian norms of the one-dimensional marginals of
X
can be used as proxies for their standard deviations. Note that the reverse inequality
\operatorname{Var}\left(\langle X,x\rangle\right)^{1/2}\leq K\left\|\langle X,x\rangle\right\|_{\psi_{2}}
for
K=\sqrt{2}
is always satisfied when
X
has mean
0
(Proposition 2.5.2. (ii) of
Vershynin (2018)
). Moreover, for a Gaussian random vector
X
, this proxy assumption holds with
K_{X}=1
. If
X
is a zero-mean sub-Gaussian random vector that satisfies the variance-proxy condition and has covariance matrix
\Sigma_{X}
, it follows from the definition above that
\left\|X\right\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq K_{X}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2}^{1/2}
. Additionally, it is straightforward to show that
\operatorname{max}_{i}(\left\|X_{i}\right\|_{\psi_{2}})\leq K_{X}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}
, where
X_{i}
is the
i
th entry of
X
. The proxy assumption allows us to compare sub-Gaussian norms of vectors to one another through their variances.

Throughout our proofs, we assume that the variance-proxy condition applies to the random vectors
X
,
B^{\top}X
,
Y
,
\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}Y
, and
\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}Y
. To simplify our assumptions, for the main theorems in this section, we conveniently assume that
X
and
Y
are strict sub-Gaussians, as defined in
(
Kereta and Klock, 2021
)
:

Definition C.2
.

A sub-Gaussian random vector
X
is called strict sub-Gaussian if there exists a constant
K_{X}
such that for any matrix
U\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times p}
, the following inequality is satisfied:

\left\|UX\right\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq K_{X}\left\|\Sigma_{UX}\right\|_{2}^{1/2}.

(S42)

C.3
Proof of Theorem
3.1

Recall that the matrices
\mathbb{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times p}
and
\mathbb{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d}
consist of
N
samples of the random vectors
X\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, respectively. We assume that
\Sigma_{X}
and
\Sigma_{Y}
are invertible, and without loss of generality, we assume that
X
and
Y
have mean
0
.

To estimate
\Sigma_{X}
and
\Sigma_{Y}
, we use their respective sample covariance estimates
\hat{\Sigma}_{Y}=\mathbb{Y}^{\top}\mathbb{Y}/N
and
\hat{\Sigma}_{X}=\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}/N
. Define
B=\Sigma_{X}^{-1}\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}
, let
\hat{B}
be the solution to the sample group lasso problem (
9
), and let
\lambda
be the associated penalization constant. In the setting of Theorem
1.1
, if we define
\tilde{H}
by the eigendecomposition
B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\equiv\tilde{H}D^{2}\tilde{H}^{\top}
, then by letting

\displaystyle T=B\tilde{H}D^{-1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times d},

(S43)

\displaystyle H=\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\tilde{H}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d},

(S44)

it follows that the
k
th column of
H
,
\eta_{k}
, is the
k
th canonical vector associated with
Y
, and the
k
th column of
T
,
\theta_{k}
, is the
k
th canonical vector associated with
X
. Moreover, the diagonal entries of
D^{2}
are the squared population canonical correlations
\gamma_{1}>\ldots>\gamma_{d}
, which we assume are distinct. This allows us to focus on estimating individual canonical vectors rather than subspaces spanned by canonical vectors sharing identical correlations.

We denote the columns of
\tilde{H}
as
\tilde{\eta}_{k}
and denote by
\{\hat{\theta}_{k}\}
and
\{\hat{\eta}_{k}\}
the estimates of the canonical vectors, and by
\{\hat{\gamma}_{k}\}
the estimated canonical correlations, that is, the diagonal entries of
\hat{D}
. Note that by definition, the squared population correlations
\gamma_{1}^{2}\ldots\gamma_{d}^{2}
are the eigenvalues of
B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B
and the estimated squared correlations
\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{2},\ldots\hat{\gamma}_{d}^{2}
are the eigenvalues of
\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}
. In the remainder of this section, we derive bounds on the estimation error for the canonical correlations, quantified by
|\gamma_{k}^{2}-\hat{\gamma}_{k}^{2}|
, and the canonical vectors, quantified by
\left\|\eta_{k}-\hat{\eta}_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}
and
\left\|\theta_{k}-\hat{\theta}_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}
.

C.3.1
Deterministic bounds

We begin by presenting our deterministic results. To establish fast-rate bounds, we use the Group restricted eigenvalue condition, analogously to the lasso regression problem
(
Hastie et al., 2015
)
and similar to
Gaynanova (2020)
in the context of penalized optimal scoring.

Definition C.3

(Group restricted eigenvalue condition)
.

A matrix
Q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{q\times p}
satisfies the Group restricted eigenvalue condition RE
(s,c,d)
with parameter
\kappa
if for all sets
S\subset\{1,\ldots p\}
with
|S|\leq s
, we have that, for all
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times d}
such that
\left\|A_{\bar{S}}\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\leq c\left\|A_{S}\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
,

\lVert QA\lVert_{F}\geq\frac{\lVert A_{S}\lVert_{F}^{2}}{\kappa}.

(S45)

Here,
|S|
denotes the cardinality of
S
, and
\bar{S}=\{1,\ldots p\}\backslash S
.

The following lemma establishes a deterministic bound for the
2
-norm of the difference between the linear operators
B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B
and
\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}
. In turn, this quantity will be used to bound the errors
|\gamma_{k}^{2}-\hat{\gamma}_{k}^{2}|
,
\left\|\eta_{k}-\hat{\eta}_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}
and
\left\|\theta_{k}-\hat{\theta}_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}
.

Lemma C.1
.

The following inequality holds:

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left\|\mathbb{X}B\right\|_{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left\|\mathbb{X}(\hat{B}-B)\right\|_{F}+\left\|B^{\top}(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X})B\right\|_{2}+\gamma_{1}\left\|(\hat{B}-B)^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+{\frac{1}{N}}\left\|\mathbb{X}(\hat{B}-B)\right\|_{F}^{2}+\left\|\hat{B}-B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\left\|(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X})B\right\|_{2,\infty}.

In the equation above, the first-order terms appear on the first line, while the second-order terms appear on the second line of the equation. In this section, wherever possible, we will keep the convention.

Let
E=\mathbb{Y}\hat{\Sigma}_{Y}^{-1/2}-\mathbb{X}B
. Next, we derive ‘slow’- and ‘fast’-rate deterministic bounds.

Lemma C.2
.

If
\lambda\geq\frac{2}{N}\left\|\mathbb{X}^{\top}E\right\|_{2,\infty}
, then the following slow-rate bound holds:

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left\|\mathbb{X}B\right\|_{2}\sqrt{\lambda}\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}^{1/2}+\left\|B^{\top}(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X})B\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\gamma_{1}\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}^{1/2}\left(\lambda\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}+\left\|\hat{\Sigma}_{X}-\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{\operatorname{max}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\lambda\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}+\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\left\|(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X})B\right\|_{2,\infty}.

If, additionally,
B
has at most
s
non-zero rows, and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\mathbb{X}
satisfies the Group restricted eigenvalue condition
RE
(s,3,d)
with parameter
\kappa_{X}
, then the following fast-rate bound holds:

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left\|\mathbb{X}B\right\|_{2}\kappa_{X}^{1/2}s^{1/2}\lambda+\left\|B^{\top}(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X})B\right\|_{2}+\gamma_{1}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2}^{1/2}\kappa_{X}s^{1/2}\lambda

\displaystyle+\kappa_{X}s\lambda^{2}+\left\|(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X})B\right\|_{2,\infty}\kappa_{X}s\lambda.

Note that in the fast-rate bound
\sqrt{\lambda}
and
\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
are replaced with
\lambda
and
\kappa_{X}s
, respectively. Next, we derive a bound for
\frac{1}{N}\left\|\mathbb{X}^{\top}E\right\|_{2,\infty}
.

Lemma C.3
.

The following inequality holds:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\left\|\mathbb{X}^{\top}E\right\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\left(\hat{\Sigma}_{XY}-\Sigma_{XY}\right)\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2,\infty}+\left\|\Sigma_{XY}\left(\hat{\Sigma}_{Y}^{-1/2}-\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\right)\right\|_{2,\infty}+\left\|\left(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\right)B\right\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle+\left\|\left(\hat{\Sigma}_{XY}-\Sigma_{XY}\right)\left(\hat{\Sigma}_{Y}^{-1/2}-\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\right)\right\|_{2,\infty}.

Denote the right-hand side of the equation in Lemma
C.3
as
\lambda_{0}
. Given that
\frac{2}{N}\left\|\mathbb{X}^{\top}E\right\|_{2,\infty}\leq 2\lambda_{0}
, choosing
\lambda\geq 2\lambda_{0}
ensures that
\lambda\geq\frac{2}{N}\left\|\mathbb{X}^{\top}E\right\|_{2,\infty}
. Thus, we can replace the assumption
\lambda\geq\frac{2}{N}\left\|\mathbb{X}^{\top}E\right\|_{2,\infty}
with the assumption
\lambda\geq 2\lambda_{0}
. Later, we will establish a high-probability bound for
\lambda_{0}
.

Due to the fact that
\left\|\left(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\right)B\right\|_{2,\infty}\leq\lambda_{0}
, we obtain the following simplification of Lemma
C.2
, where the fourth and fifth terms are combined.

Lemma C.4
.

If
\lambda\geq 2\lambda_{0}
, then the following slow-rate bound holds:

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left\|\mathbb{X}B\right\|_{2}\sqrt{\lambda}\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}^{1/2}+\left\|B^{\top}(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X})B\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\gamma_{1}\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}^{1/2}\left(\lambda\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}+\left\|\hat{\Sigma}_{X}-\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{\operatorname{max}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\lambda\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}.

If, additionally,
B
has at most
s
non-zero rows, and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\mathbb{X}
satisfies the Group restricted eigenvalue condition
RE
(s,3,d)
with parameter
\kappa_{X}
, then the following fast-rate bound holds:

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left\|\mathbb{X}B\right\|_{2}\kappa_{X}^{1/2}s^{1/2}\lambda+\left\|B^{\top}(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X})B\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\gamma_{1}\kappa_{X}^{1/2}s^{1/2}\left(1+\kappa_{X}s\left\|\hat{\Sigma}_{X}-\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{\operatorname{max}}\right)^{1/2}\lambda

\displaystyle+\kappa_{X}s\lambda^{2}.

Lemma
C.4
shows that the rate of convergence will ultimately be determined by
\lambda_{0}
,
\left\|B^{\top}(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X})B\right\|_{2}
, and
\left\|\hat{\Sigma}_{X}-\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{\operatorname{max}}
.

C.3.2
Probabilistic bounds

From now on, we assume that
X
and
Y
are sub-Gaussian random vectors and that the variance-proxy condition in Definition
C.1
holds for the random vectors
X
,
B^{\top}X
,
Y
,
\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}Y
, and
\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}Y
. We will repeatedly use the union bound and omit for simplicity the absolute constants arising from its applications.

First, we present an intermediary result that will be used to derive a probabilistic upper bound for
\lambda_{0}
.

Lemma C.5
.

Let
X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and
Z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be zero-mean random vectors with covariance matrices
\Sigma_{X}
and
\Sigma_{Z}
and cross-covariance matrix
\Sigma_{XZ}
. Assume the entries of
X
and
Z
are sub-Gaussian random variables with norms
\left\|X_{i}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}=g_{i}
and
\left\|Z_{j}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}=h_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots p
and
j=1,\ldots d
. Let
g=\operatorname{max}(g_{i})
and
h=\operatorname{max}(h_{j})
. Let
\mathbb{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times p}
and
\mathbb{Z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times d}
be data matrices such that the pairs of rows
\{(\mathbb{X}_{i}^{\top},\mathbb{Z}_{i}^{\top})\}
are independent samples from the joint distribution
(X,Z)
.
If
d\leq p
and
\operatorname{log}(p)=o(N)
, then for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\eta
,

\left\|\Sigma_{XZ}-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{Z}\right\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim gh\sqrt{\frac{d}{N}\operatorname{log}(p\eta^{-1})}.

(S46)

Remark 7
.

In Lemma
C.5
, it is stated that for a fixed
\eta
, if
\lim\left(\operatorname{log}(p)/N\right)=0
as
p
and
N
go to infinity, then, eventually, the stated bound holds.

Next, we derive probabilistic upper bounds for
\lambda_{0}
, bounding the terms in Lemma
C.3
.

Lemma C.6
.

If
d\leq p
and
\operatorname{log}(p)=o(N)
, then for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability
1-\eta
,

\left\|\left(\hat{\Sigma}_{XY}-\Sigma_{XY}\right)\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\operatorname{max}_{i}(\left\|X_{i}\right\|_{\psi_{2}})\sqrt{\frac{d}{N}\operatorname{log}(p\eta^{-1})}

(S47)

and

\left\|\left(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\right)B\right\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\operatorname{max}_{i}(\left\|X_{i}\right\|_{\psi_{2}})\gamma_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d}{N}\operatorname{log}(p\eta^{-1})}.

(S48)

Moreover, if
d=o(N)
, then

\left\|\Sigma_{XY}\left(\hat{\Sigma}_{Y}^{-1/2}-\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\right)\right\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}\gamma_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d+\operatorname{log}(\eta^{-1})}{N}}

(S49)

and

\left\|\left(\hat{\Sigma}_{XY}-\Sigma_{XY}\right)\left(\hat{\Sigma}_{Y}^{-1/2}-\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\right)\right\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\left\|\left(\hat{\Sigma}_{XY}-\Sigma_{XY}\right)\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2,\infty}.

(S50)

Remark 8
.

As noted in Section
C.1
,
\operatorname{max}_{i}(\left\|X_{i}\right\|_{\psi_{2}})\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}
.

Using Lemmas
C.3
and
C.6
, and Remark
8
, it straightforward to derive the following result.

Lemma C.7
.

If
d\leq p
,
\operatorname{log}(p)=o(N)
, and
d=o(N)
, then for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability
1-\eta
,

\lambda_{0}\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{d}{N}\operatorname{log}(p\eta^{-1})}.

(S51)

Next, we establish bounds on the other terms appearing in
C.4
.

Lemma C.8
.

If
\operatorname{log}(p)=o(N)
, then for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability
1-\eta
,

\left\|\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\right\|_{\operatorname{max}}\lesssim\operatorname{max}(\left\|X_{i}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2})\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{log}\left(p\eta^{-1}\right)}{N}}.

(S52)

If
d=o(N)
, then for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability
1-\eta
,

\left\|B^{\top}(\Sigma_{X}-\hat{\Sigma}_{X})B\right\|_{2}\lesssim\gamma_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{d+\operatorname{log}\left(\eta^{-1}\right)}{N}},

(S53)

and

\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left\|\mathbb{X}B\right\|_{2}\lesssim\gamma_{1}.

(S54)

Before presenting our final bounds, we establish that the group-restricted eigenvalue condition holds for the design matrix
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\mathbb{X}
, with high probability, assuming that the same condition holds for
\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}
.

Lemma C.9
.

Suppose
\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}
satisfies the group restricted eigenvalue condition RE
(s,3,d)
with parameter
\kappa=\kappa(s,d,\Sigma_{X}^{1/2})
. If
\operatorname{max}_{i=1,\ldots p}(\left\|X_{i}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{4})\kappa^{2}s^{2}\log\left(p\right)=o(N)
and
s^{2}\operatorname{log}(p)=o(N)
, then for any fixed
\eta
, with probability
1-\eta
,
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\mathbb{X}
satisfies the group restricted eigenvalue condition RE
(s,3,d)
with parameter
\kappa_{X}
, where

0<\kappa_{X}\leq 2\kappa.

(S55)

Next, we state our probabilistic bound for
\left\|\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}
. The proof of the slow-rate bound follows straightforwardly from Lemmas
C.4
,
C.7
and
C.8
. The proof of the fast-rate bound follows similarly from Lemmas
C.4
,
C.7
and
C.8
, with the addition of Lemma
C.9
.

Theorem C.1
.

Assume
X
and
Y
are sub-Gaussian random vectors and that
X
,
B^{\top}X
,
\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}Y
satisfy the variance-proxy condition
C.1
. Moreover, assume that
d\leq p
,
\log(p)=o(N)
, and
d=o(N)
. Fix
\eta\in(0,1)
, and for some absolute constant
C>0
, let
\lambda=C\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{d}{N}\log(p\eta^{-1})}
. Then, with probability
1-\eta
, the following slow-rate bound holds:

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d}{N}\log(p\eta^{-1})\right)^{1/4}\left[\gamma_{1}^{2}+\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}+\gamma_{1}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/4}\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}^{1/2}\left(1+\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}^{1/2}+\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/4}\right)\right].

Under the additional assumption that
B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times d}
has at most
s
nonzero rows,
\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}
satisfies the group restricted eigenvalue condition RE
(s,3,d)
with parameter
\kappa=\kappa(s,d,\Sigma_{X}^{1/2})
,
s^{2}\log(p)=o(N)
, and
\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{2}\kappa^{2}s^{2}\log(p)=o(N)
, then the following fast-rate bound holds:

\left\|\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{d}{N}\log\left(p\eta^{-1}\right)\right)^{1/2}\left[\gamma_{1}\left(\gamma_{1}+\kappa^{1/2}s^{1/2}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}\right)\right].

(S56)

Corollary C.1
.

In the setting of Theorem
C.1
, under the additional assumption that
\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty},\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\geq 1
, then the expression of the slow-rate bound simplifies as follows:

\left\|\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{d}{N}\log\left(p\eta^{-1}\right)\right)^{1/4}\left[\gamma_{1}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\right].

(S57)

Under the additional assumption that
\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty},\kappa\geq 1
, then the expression of the fast-rate bound simplifies as follows:

\left\|\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{d}{N}\log\left(p\eta^{-1}\right)\right)^{1/2}\left[\gamma_{1}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}s^{1/2}\kappa^{1/2}\right].

(S58)

Remark 9
.

The eigenvalues of the matrices
\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}
and
B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B
are
\{\hat{\gamma}_{k}^{2}\}
and
\{\gamma_{k}^{2}\}
respectively. Then by Weyl’s inequality (
Bhatia (2013)
Corollary III.2.6.), because we have bounded the operator norm of the difference between these two matrices, we immediately obtain bounds on the estimation error of
\gamma_{k}^{2}
by
\hat{\gamma}_{k}^{2}
in Algorithm
2
.

To establish bounds for
\left\|\theta_{k}-\hat{\theta}_{k}\right\|_{2}
and
\left\|\eta_{k}-\hat{\eta}_{k}\right\|_{2}
, we first introduce a few supporting lemmas.

Lemma C.10
.

Under the slow-rate bound assumptions stated in Corollary
C.1
, for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\eta
, we have

\left\|B-\hat{B}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{d}{N}\log\left(p\eta^{-1}\right)\right)^{1/4}\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}.

(S59)

Under the fast-rate bound assumptions stated in Corollary
C.1
, for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\eta
, we have

\left\|B-\hat{B}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{d}{N}\log\left(p\eta^{-1}\right)\right)^{1/2}\kappa s^{1/2}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}.

(S60)

Lemma C.11
.

Suppose that
Y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is a sub-Gaussian vector,
d=o(N)
and that
Y
satisfies the variance-proxy condition. Then, for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability
1-\eta
, we have

\left\|\Sigma_{Y}-\hat{\Sigma}_{Y}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d\log\left(\eta^{-1}\right)}{N}}.

(S61)

Additionally, suppose that
\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}Y
satisfies the variance-proxy condition, and
\left\|\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}d=o(N)
. Then for fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability
1-\eta
, we have

\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}-\hat{\Sigma}_{Y}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{d\log\left(\eta^{-1}\right)}{N}}.

(S62)

Studying the theoretical properties of CCA through the lens of regression, using the matrix
B
, has been convenient thus far. However, for our final results, we bound
B=\Sigma_{X}^{-1/2}\tilde{T}D\tilde{H}=TD\tilde{H}
in terms of quantities that are more directly related to the CCA problem. Using identity
12
in Section
F
, along with the standard properties of the
2
-norm, and noting that
\tilde{T}
,
\tilde{H}
are orthogonal matrices and
D
is a diagonal matrix with diagonal values no greater than
1
, we observe that

\displaystyle\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\leq

\displaystyle\left\|T\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\leq\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{-1/2}\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}},

(S63)

\displaystyle\left\|B\right\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle\left\|T\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}.

(S64)

Hence, in Corollary
C.1
, we can replace the assumption that
\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\geq 1
with the assumption that
\left\|T\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\geq 1
.

Next, we state our probabilistic bounds on the estimated canonical vectors. We denote with
K=\operatorname{max}\left\{i\in\{1,\ldots d\}:\gamma_{i}>0\right\}
the number of nontrivial canonical vectors. Moreover, to simplify the notation, we use the conventions
\gamma_{K+1}^{2}=-\infty
and
\gamma_{0}^{2}=\infty
.

Theorem C.2
.

Under the slow-rate bound assumptions stated in Corollary
C.1
and assuming that the canonical correlations
\gamma_{1},\ldots\gamma_{K}
are bounded from below,
Y
and
\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}Y
satisfy the variance-proxy condition, and
\hat{\eta}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}_{Y}^{1/2}\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}\eta\geq 0
, for
k=1,\ldots K
.

If
d\log\left(p\right)\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{2}\left\|T\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}^{4}=o(N)
, then, for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability
1-\eta
,

\left\|\theta_{k}-\hat{\theta}_{k}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{d}{N}\log\left(p\eta^{-1}\right)\right)^{1/4}\frac{\gamma_{1}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}\left\|T\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}}{\gamma_{k}\operatorname{min}\left(\gamma_{k-1}^{2}-\gamma_{k}^{2},\gamma_{k}^{2}-\gamma_{k+1}^{2}\right)},\qquad k=1,\ldots K.

(S65)

If
\left\|\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}d=o(N)
and
\left\|\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}d=o(N)
, then, for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability
1-\eta
,

\left\|\eta_{k}-\hat{\eta}_{k}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{d}{N}\log\left(p\eta^{-1}\right)\right)^{1/4}\frac{\gamma_{1}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}\left\|T\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}}{\operatorname{min}\left(\gamma_{k-1}^{2}-\gamma_{k}^{2},\gamma_{k}^{2}-\gamma_{k+1}^{2}\right)},\qquad k=1,\ldots K.

(S66)

Theorem C.3
.

Under the fast-rate bound assumptions stated in Corollary
C.1
and assuming that the canonical correlations
\gamma_{1},\ldots\gamma_{K}
are bounded from below,
Y
and
\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}Y
satisfy the variance-proxy condition, and
\hat{\eta}_{k}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}_{Y}^{1/2}\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}\eta_{k}\geq 0
, for
k=1,\ldots K
.

If
d\log\left(p\right)\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}s\kappa=o(N)
, then for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability
1-\eta
,

\left\|\theta_{k}-\hat{\theta}_{k}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{d}{N}\log\left(p\eta^{-1}\right)\right)^{1/2}\frac{\gamma_{1}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}\left\|T\right\|_{2}s^{1/2}\kappa}{\gamma_{k}\operatorname{min}\left(\gamma_{k-1}^{2}-\gamma_{k}^{2},\gamma_{k}^{2}-\gamma_{k+1}^{2}\right)},\qquad k=1,\ldots K.

(S67)

If
\left\|\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}d=o(N)
, then for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
, with probability
1-\eta
,

\left\|\eta_{k}-\hat{\eta}_{k}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{d}{N}\log\left(p\eta^{-1}\right)\right)^{1/2}\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\operatorname{max}\left\{\frac{\gamma_{1}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{1/2}s^{1/2}\kappa^{1/2}}{\operatorname{min}\left(\gamma_{k-1}^{2}-\gamma_{k}^{2},\gamma_{k}^{2}-\gamma_{k+1}^{2}\right)},\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\right\},

(S68)

with
k=1,\ldots K
.

C.4
Proofs for the deterministic bounds in Section
C.3.1
and for the probabilistic bounds in Section
C.3.2

Proof of Lemma
C.1
:

The triangle inequality is used repeatedly without comment. By adding and subtracting
\hat{B}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B
, we have

\left\|\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\hat{B}^{T}(\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-\Sigma_{X}B)\right\|_{2}+\left\|(\hat{B}-B)^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}.

(S69)

Since

\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-\Sigma_{X}B=\hat{\Sigma}_{X}(\hat{B}-B)+(\hat{\Sigma}_{X}-\Sigma_{X})B

(S70)

by adding and subtracting
\hat{\Sigma}_{X}B
, we deduce that

\left\|\hat{B}^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}-B^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}\leq\underbrace{\left\|\hat{B}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}(\hat{B}-B)\right\|_{2}}_{\text{Term I}}+\underbrace{\left\|\hat{B}^{\top}(\hat{\Sigma}_{X}-\Sigma_{X})B\right\|_{2}}_{\text{Term II}}+\underbrace{\left\|(\hat{B}-B)^{\top}\Sigma_{X}B\right\|_{2}}_{\text{Term III}}.

(S71)

We bound each term individually. Recall that
\hat{\Sigma}_{X}=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}
.

Term I: We have

\left\|\hat{B}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}(\hat{B}-B)\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left\|\mathbb{X}\hat{B}\right\|_{2}\left\|\mathbb{X}(\hat{B}-B)\right\|_{F}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}

(S72)

using
\left\|AB\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|A\right\|_{2}\left\|B\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|A\right\|_{2}\left\|B\right\|_{F}
.
Since

\left\|\mathbb{X}\hat{B}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\mathbb{X}B\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathbb{X}(\hat{B}-B)\right\|_{2},

(S73)

we have
