\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{1}\delta_{\Gamma}\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{2}+\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{2}\left\|\hat{\phi}_{j}\delta_{\Gamma}\phi_{j}\right\|_{\mu}^{2},

(S228)

from which the statement of the lemma follows, and the proof is complete. ∎

Proof of Lemma
D.6
:

From
Lin and Yao (2019)
(page 3551),
\Gamma_{\hat{\mu},\mu}\hat{\phi}_{j}
are the eigenvectors of
\Phi\hat{\mathcal{C}}
. By definition, the
\phi_{j}
are the eigenvectors of
\mathcal{C}
. Thus, we can use the Davis-Kahan Theorem for Hilbert spaces
(
Jirak and Wahl, 2020
)
to obtain that

\Gamma_{\hat{\mu},\mu}\hat{\phi}_{j}-\phi_{j}\leq 2\sqrt{2}\operatorname{max}\left(\left(\omega_{j-1}-\omega_{j}\right)^{-1},\left(\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}\right)^{-1}\right)\lVert\mathcal{C}-\Phi\hat{\mathcal{C}}\lVert_{\operatorname{op}},

(S229)

where
\omega_{0}\equiv\infty
and
\lVert\mathcal{C}\lVert_{\operatorname{op}}\equiv\underset{U\in L^{2}(T\mu),\left\|U\right\|_{\mu}=1}{\operatorname{sup}\ }\left\|\mathcal{C}U\right\|_{\mu}
.
Adding and subtracting
\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu}
in
\lVert\mathcal{C}-\Phi\hat{\mathcal{C}}\lVert_{\operatorname{op}}
and using identity
18
from Section
F
, the proof is complete. ∎

Proof of Lemma
D.7
:

The first statement is equivalent to Lemma 5.2 of
Cardot et al. (1999)
.
To show the second statement, we begin with the definition of
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
and Proposition 2 item 5 of
Lin and Yao (2019)
, from which we deduce that

\Phi\hat{\mathcal{C}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\Gamma_{\hat{\mu},\mu}\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{i}\right)\otimes\left(\Gamma_{\hat{\mu},\mu}\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{i}\right).

(S230)

This implies

\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu}-\Phi\hat{\mathcal{C}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\otimes a_{i}-b_{i}\otimes b_{i},

(S231)

where we denote
a_{i}\equiv\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{i}
and
b_{i}\equiv\Gamma_{\hat{\mu},\mu}\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{i}
.
It is straightforward to show that
a\otimes a-b\otimes b=a\otimes\left(a-b\right)+\left(a-b\right)\otimes\left(b-a\right)+\left(a-b\right)\otimes a
, where
a,b\in L^{2}(T\mu)
. From Theorem 3.4.7. of
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
,
\lVert a\otimes b\lVert_{\operatorname{op}}=\lVert a\lVert_{\mu}\lVert b\lVert_{\mu}
, and therefore,

\lVert a\otimes a-b\otimes b\lVert_{\operatorname{op}}\leq 2\left\|a\right\|_{\mu}\left\|a-b\right\|_{\mu}+\left\|a-b\right\|_{\mu}^{2}.

(S232)

Then,

\lVert\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu}-\Phi\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\operatorname{op}}\lVert\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}2\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{i}\right\|_{\mu}\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{i}\delta_{\Gamma}\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{i}\right\|_{\mu}+\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{i}\delta_{\Gamma}\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{i}\right\|_{\mu}^{2},

(S233)

so that

\lVert\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu}-\Phi\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\operatorname{op}}\lVert^{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}2\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{i}\right\|_{\mu}\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{i}\delta_{\Gamma}\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{i}\right\|_{\mu}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{i}\delta_{\Gamma}\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{i}\right\|_{\mu}^{2}\right)^{2}.

(S234)

It is straightforward to show that, for any i.i.d. random variables
W_{i}
with finite variance we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}W_{i}\right)^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[W_{1}^{2}\right],

(S235)

where the
W_{i}
are not required to have mean
0
.
Taking the expectation in equation (
S234
), applying this last result on each term, using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and subsequently using the assumption stated in the Lemma concerning
\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{i}\delta_{\Gamma}\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{i}\right\|_{\mu}
, the proof is complete. ∎

Proof of Lemma
D.8
:

We start with a mean value theorem result for smooth functions
f
from
[0,t_{1}]
into a normed vector space
V
, where
t_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. By Theorem 1.1.1. of
Hörmander (2015)
, we have

\lVert f(t_{1})-f(0)\lVert=\left|b-a\right|\underset{c\in[0,t_{1}]}{\operatorname{sup}\ }f^{\prime}(c).

(S236)

We set

f(t)=\Gamma_{\gamma(t),\gamma(0)}U\left(\gamma(t)\right)-U\left(\gamma(0)\right),

(S237)

where
U
is a smooth vector field on
\mathcal{M}
,
U:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow T\mathcal{M}
, and
\gamma(t)
is the minimizing geodesic between two points
x,y\in\mathcal{M}
(
\gamma(0)=x,\gamma\left((x,y)\right)=y
. Letting
t_{1}=d(x,y)
, then
\gamma:[0,t_{1}]\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
, and
f:[0,t_{1}]\rightarrow T_{x}\mathcal{M}
. Letting
\lVert W\lVert_{x}
denote the norm of
W\in T_{x}\mathcal{M}
, and using
f(t)
in equation (
S236
), we have

\lVert\Gamma_{y,x}U(y)-U(x)\lVert\leq d(x,y)\underset{c\in[0,d(x,y)]}{\operatorname{sup}\ }\lVert f^{\prime}(c)\lVert.

(S238)

We can determine
f^{\prime}(c)
:

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(c)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{f(c+t)-f(c)}{t}

(S239)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Gamma_{\gamma(c+t),x}U\left(\gamma(c+t)\right)-U(x)-\Gamma_{\gamma(c),x}U\left(\gamma(c)\right)+U(x)}{t}

(S240)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Gamma_{\gamma(c+t),x}U\left(\gamma(c+t)\right)-\Gamma_{\gamma(c),x}U\left(\gamma(c)\right)}{t}.

(S241)

Using that
\Gamma_{z,x}U(z)=\Gamma_{y,x}\left(\Gamma_{z,y}U(z)\right)
, where
x=x
,
y=\gamma(c)
, and
z=\gamma(c+t)
, we have

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(c)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Gamma_{\gamma(c),x}\left[\Gamma_{\gamma(c+t),\gamma(c)}U\left(\gamma(c+t)\right)-U\left(\gamma(c)\right)\right]}{t}

(S242)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\gamma(c),x}\left[\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Gamma_{\gamma(c+t),\gamma(c)}U\left(\gamma(c+t)\right)-U\left(\gamma(c)\right)}{t}\right]

(S243)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\gamma(c),x}\left[\nabla_{\gamma^{\prime}(c)}U\right].

(S244)

Since for any smooth vector field
W
,
\lVert\Gamma_{y,x}\left(W\right)\lVert_{x}=\lVert W\lVert_{y}
, the proof is complete. ∎

Proof of Lemma
D.9
:

We apply Lemma
D.8
to the vector
V_{t}(p)\equiv\operatorname{Log}_{p}y_{1}(t)
for fixed
t
. We have that (equation (25) of
Kendall and Le (2011)
)

V_{t}(p)=\operatorname{grad}\left(-\frac{1}{2}d\left(p,y_{1}(t)\right)^{2}\right)=\operatorname{grad}\left(f_{t}\right)(p).

(S245)

Then, by the definition of the Riemannian Hessian
H_{t}
of
f_{t}
, for a smooth curve
\gamma
on
\mathcal{M}
at time
c
,

\lVert\nabla_{\gamma^{\prime}(c)}V_{t}\left(\gamma(c)\right)\lVert_{\gamma(c)}=\lVert\nabla_{\gamma^{\prime}(c)}\operatorname{grad}\left(f_{t}\right)(p)\lVert_{\gamma(c)}=\lVert H_{t}\left(\gamma(c)\right)\left(\gamma^{\prime}(c)\right)\lVert_{\gamma(c)}.

(S246)

Using Lemma
D.8
, we choose
x=\mu(t)
and
y=\hat{\mu}(t)
, and we denote
\gamma_{t}(s)
as the minimizing geodesic between
\mu(t)
and
\hat{\mu}(t)
:

\displaystyle\lVert\Gamma_{\hat{\mu}(t),\mu(t)}\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu(t)}}y_{1}(t)-\operatorname{Log}_{\mu(t)}y_{1}(t)\lVert

\displaystyle\leq d\left(\hat{\mu}(t),\mu(t)\right)\underset{c\in\left[0,d\left(\hat{\mu}(t),\mu(t)\right)\right]}{\operatorname{sup}\ }\lVert H_{t}\left(\gamma_{t}(c)\right)\left(\gamma_{t}^{\prime}(c)\right)\lVert_{\gamma_{t}(c)}

(S247)

\displaystyle\lesssim d\left(\hat{\mu}(t),\mu(t)\right)

(S248)

for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
with probability one, where in the last inequality we have used Assumption
3
in the Lemma statement along with the fact that, since
\gamma_{t}(s)
is a minimizing geodesic,
\lVert\gamma^{\prime}(s)\lVert_{\gamma(s)}=1
for all
s
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{1}\delta_{\Gamma}\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\lVert_{\mu}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\mathcal{T}}\lVert\Gamma_{\hat{\mu}(t),\mu(t)}\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu(t)}}y_{1}(t)-\operatorname{Log}_{\mu(t)}y_{1}(t)\lVert^{2}dt\right]

(S249)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\mathcal{T}}d\left(\hat{\mu}(t),\mu(t)\right)^{2}dt\right].

(S250)

By Tonelli’s theorem,

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\mathcal{T}}d\left(\hat{\mu}(t),\mu(t)\right)^{2}dt\right]=\int_{\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}\left[d\left(\hat{\mu}(t),\mu(t)\right)^{2}\right]dt.

(S251)

Next, we will apply Corollary 4 of
Schötz (2019)
to bound
\mathbb{E}\left[d\left(\hat{\mu}(t),\mu(t)\right)^{2}\right]
. To do so, we need the following definitions related to the metric entropy of geodesic balls in
\mathcal{M}
. Let
B_{\delta}(a)\equiv\left\{x\in\mathcal{M}:d(x,a)\leq\delta\right\}
be the ball of radius
r
centered at
a
on
\mathcal{M}
, and
N(B,r)\equiv\operatorname{min}\left(k\in\mathbb{N}|\exists q_{1},\ldots q_{k}\in\mathcal{M}:B\subseteq\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}B_{r}(q_{j})\right)
be the covering number of a set
B
using radius
r
. Then, the entropy assumption in the statement of Corollary 4 of
Schötz (2019)
, that there exists
0<\beta<1
such that, for all
\delta,r>0
,
\log\left(N\left(B_{\delta}\left(\mu(t)\right),r\right)\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left(\frac{\delta}{r}\right)^{\beta}
, is satisfied (
Ahidar-Coutrix et al. (2020)
Example 2.3).

Now additionally using Assumption
1
made in the statement of the Lemma, we can apply Corollary 4 of
Schötz (2019)
with
\varepsilon=1
to obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[d\left(\hat{\mu}(t),\mu(t)\right)^{2}\right]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[d\left(y_{1}(t),y_{2}(t)\right)^{3}\right]^{2/3}\frac{1}{N}

(S252)

for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
.

Using Assumption
2
made in the statement of the Lemma, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[d\left(\hat{\mu}(t),\mu(t)\right)^{2}\right]\lesssim\frac{1}{N},

(S253)

which we can combine with equation (
S250
) to establish

\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{1}\delta_{\Gamma}\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\lVert_{\mu}^{2}\right]\lesssim\int_{\mathcal{T}}\frac{1}{N}dt=\frac{\tau}{N},

(S254)

completing the proof. ∎

Proof of Lemma
D.12
:

Note that
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{\top}Y\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\operatorname{tr}\left(YY^{\top}\right)\right]=\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[YY^{\top}\right]\right)=\operatorname{tr}\left(\Sigma_{Y}\right)
. Then, the first two statements of the Lemma follow from observing that
\Sigma_{Y}
is diagonal with entries
\omega_{j}
, the eigenvalues of the covariance operator of
\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y
, by the results of Lemma
B.1
.

To show
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]
, we begin with the triangle inequality:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z-Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right].

(S255)

Using Lemma
D.11
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}
, we observe for
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]
to hold, it is necessary that
\frac{d}{N}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\right)^{-1}\lesssim 1
. This follows from assumption (
S185
) provided in the Lemma, because under the other assumptions provided in the Lemma, both
\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}\right)/{\omega_{d}^{2}}
and
\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}
are greater than or equal to 1. From this, we also deduce that
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z-Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]=o(1)
.

To show that
\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}
, a more involved argument is required. We again begin with the triangle inequality:

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}-\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}

(S256)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Z}-\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(S257)

This implies

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Z}-\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2},

(S258)

so that

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left(1-\left\|\Sigma_{Z}-\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\leq\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2},

(S259)

and therefore, if
\left\|\Sigma_{Z}-\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}=o(1)
held, then we would have
\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}
and the proof would be complete. Thus, to show
\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}
, it suffices to show that
\frac{d}{N}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}}{\omega_{d}^{2}}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}=o(1)
implies
\left\|\Sigma_{Z}-\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}=o(1)
. From the second item of Lemma
D.3
and from
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]
which has already been shown, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma_{Z}-\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{max}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z\right\|_{2}^{2}\right],\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z-Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]

(S260)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z-Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}.

(S261)

Thus, for
\left\|\Sigma_{Z}-\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}=o(1)
to hold, it suffices to show that
\frac{1}{\omega_{d}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z-Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}=o(1)
. From Lemma
D.11
, we have made exactly the assumption so that
\frac{1}{\omega_{d}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z-Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}=o(1)
holds, completing the proof that
\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}
.

To show the final statement of the Lemma, that
\left\|\Sigma_{Z}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}
, we can make an argument similar to the one used to show
\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}
. From this, it suffices to show that
\frac{d}{N}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}}{\omega_{1}^{2}}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}=o(1)
. Since
\omega_{d}\leq\omega_{1}
,
\frac{d}{N}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}}{\omega_{1}^{2}}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}=o(1)
holds under our assumptions and the proof is complete. ∎

Proof of Lemma
D.14
:

To bound
|\gamma_{k}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k}|
, we can use Weyl’s inequality (
Bhatia (2013)
Corollary III.2.6.), because
\gamma_{k}
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{k}
are the
k
eigenvalues of the matrices
|C|
and
|A|
respectively
(
Uurtio et al., 2018
)
:

|\gamma_{k}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k}|\leq\left\|\left|A\right|-\left|C\right|\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\left|A\right|-\left|C\right|\right\|_{F}\lesssim\left\|A-C\right\|_{F},

(S262)

where in the second inequality we have used identity
19
from Section
F
. Combining Lemmas

D.11
,
D.12
, and
D.13
, we observe that the stated assumption in the Lemma implies
|\gamma_{k}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k}|\lesssim\min\{\gamma_{k},\tilde{\gamma}_{k}\}
, establishing the stated conclusion using the assumption that
\gamma_{k}
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{k}
are bounded from below. That
\gamma_{k}^{2}
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2}
are asymptotically equivalent follows from the same argument, in addition to the function
f(x)=x^{2}
being Lipschitz continuous on the interval
[0,1]
.

Appendix E
Asymmetric Sparse-Functional CCA: Proof of Theorem
3.3

In this section we prove Theorem
3.3
, which shows that our sample estimates of the canonical variables, derived from our proposed estimates of the canonical vectors, grow asymptotically close to the population canonical variable solutions of the infinite-dimensional CCA problem in Theorem
B.1
. We denote by
\left(U_{k},V_{k}\right)
the
k
th canonical variable solution pair to the infinite-dimensional problem in Theorem
B.1
. We denote by
\left\{\gamma_{k}^{*}\right\}_{k=1}^{p}
the canonical correlations that are attained by these solution pairs. We denote with
K=\operatorname{max}\left\{i\in\{1,\ldots p\}:\gamma_{i}^{*}>0\right\}
the number of nontrivial canonical vectors in this problem, and to simplify the notation, we use the convention
{\gamma_{0}^{*}}^{2}=\infty
and
{\gamma_{K+1}^{*}}^{2}=-\infty
. We often refer to canonical variables as canonical scores or simply scores, which are distinct from the principal scores derived from the principal component expansion of
\chi_{1}
. The difference will be clear from context, but in general, ‘scores’ with no preceding modifier refers to the canonical scores.

Since we are showing that two random variables are close, we need to specify the probability spaces and random variables of interest. In this section we suppose that we observe realizations of
\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}
which is used in the Sparse-Functional Asymmetric CCA (Algorithm
1
). We assume these are i.i.d samples that all live on the probability space
\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}\right)
.
d
denotes the number of principal components we choose to approximate
\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y
by. We obtain estimates of
\hat{\mu}
,
\left\{\hat{\phi}_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{d}
,
\hat{B}
,
\left\{\hat{\omega}_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{d}
,
\left\{\hat{\gamma}_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{d}
,
\hat{\Sigma}_{Y},\hat{\Sigma}_{X}
,
\hat{H}=\left[\hat{\eta}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\eta}_{d}\right]
,
\hat{T}=\left[\hat{\theta}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\theta}_{d}\right]
, and
\hat{\psi}_{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\hat{\eta}_{kj}\hat{\phi}_{j}
for
k=1,\ldots d
.

We will define the scores from the sample procedure as follows. Observing a new and independent data point
\left(X_{\text{test}},y_{\text{test}}\right)
from the same distribution as the sample, which also lives on
\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}\right)
, we define

\left(\hat{U}_{k},\hat{V}_{k}\right)\equiv\left(\llangle\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{\text{test}},\hat{\psi}_{k}\rrangle_{\hat{\mu}},\hat{\theta}_{k}^{\top}X_{\text{test}}\right),

(S263)

which are the canonical scores we would obtain from the new data point and our sample canonical vector estimates.
To be precise, we denote by
\left(U_{k},V_{k}\right)
the
k
th canonical variable solution pair to the infinite-dimensional problem in Theorem
B.1
, between
\chi_{1}=\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{\text{test}}
and
\chi_{2}=X_{\text{test}}
, for
k=1,\ldots K
. We would like to show that
\hat{U}_{k}
becomes close to
U_{k}
and
\hat{V}_{k}
becomes close to
V_{k}
in an asymptotic sense.

Throughout the proof, we make the same assumptions in Section
D.3
, with the important exception that, we no longer make a finite-dimensional correlation structure Assumption
B.1
between
X
and
y
. Thus, we allow for all principal scores of the functional data
y
to potentially be correlated with the components of
X
.

The main idea of the proof of the bounds is similar to that of the canonical vectors, and indeed we rely on the results of the previous supplementary sections. We define several intermediate scores in order to show that the sample score and the infinite-dimensional score are close together.

The first intermediate scores come from the population
d
-dimensional canonical correlation problem described in Theorem
B.2
and equation (
S19
), which we denote as
\left(U_{k}^{(d)},V_{k}^{(d)}\right)
. By Theorem
B.2
, these can equivalently be derived from the canonical vector solutions given by the following CCA problem:

\displaystyle(\eta_{1},\theta_{1})

\displaystyle=\underset{\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p},\operatorname{Var}(\eta^{\top}Y_{\text{test}})=\operatorname{Var}(\theta^{\top}X_{\text{test}})=1}{\operatorname{arg\,max\,}}\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(\eta^{\top}Y_{\text{test}},\theta^{\top}X_{\text{test}}\right),

(S264)

\displaystyle(\eta_{k},\theta_{k})

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p},\operatorname{Var}(\eta^{\top}Y_{\text{test}})=\operatorname{Var}(\theta^{\top}X_{\text{test}})=1\\
\operatorname{Cov}\left(\eta^{\top}Y_{\text{test}},\eta_{i}^{\top}Y_{\text{test}}\right)=0,i=1,\ldots,k\\
\operatorname{Cov}\left(\theta^{\top}X_{\text{test}},\theta_{i}^{\top}X_{\text{test}}\right)=0,i=1,\ldots,k\end{subarray}}{\operatorname{arg\,max\,}}\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(\eta^{\top}Y_{\text{test}},\theta^{\top}X_{\text{test}}\right),\qquad k=2,\ldots K

(S265)

Here,
Y_{\text{test}}
is the
d
-dimensional random vector such that
\left(Y_{\text{test}}\right)_{j}=\llangle\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{\text{test}},\phi_{j}\rrangle_{\mu}
, the
j
th principal score associated with the population principal component
\phi_{j}
of
\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{\text{test}}
, for
j=1,\ldots d
. By definition,
\left(U_{k}^{(d)},V_{k}^{(d)}\right)=\left(\eta_{k}^{\top}Y_{\text{test}},\theta_{k}^{\top}X_{\text{test}}\right)
. We refer to
\left\{\gamma_{k}\right\}_{k=1}^{K}
as the canonical correlations attained in this problem.

The second intermediate scores are derived from the population canonical correlation problem derived from the ‘estimates’ of
Y
that we make when using
\hat{\mu}
and
\hat{\phi}_{j}
:

\displaystyle(a_{1},b_{1})

\displaystyle=\underset{a\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},b\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p},\operatorname{Var}(a^{\top}Z)=\operatorname{Var}(b^{\top}X)=1}{\operatorname{arg\,max\,}}\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(a^{\top}Z,b^{\top}X\right),

(S266)

\displaystyle(a_{k},b_{k})

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},b\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p},\operatorname{Var}(a^{\top}Z)=\operatorname{Var}(b^{\top}X)=1\\
\operatorname{Cov}\left(a^{\top}Z,a_{i}^{\top}Z\right)=0,i=1,\ldots,k\\
\operatorname{Cov}\left(b^{\top}X,b_{i}^{\top}X\right)=0,i=1,\ldots,k\end{subarray}}{\operatorname{arg\,max\,}}\operatorname{Corr}^{2}\left(a^{\top}Z,b^{\top}X\right),\qquad k=2,\ldots K.

(S267)

Here,
Z
is defined as

\left(Z\right)_{j}\equiv\llangle\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{1},\hat{\phi}_{j}\rrangle_{\hat{\mu}}.

(S268)

We let
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{2}\ldots\tilde{\gamma}_{K}^{2}
denote the squared canonical correlations attained by the pairs
\left(a_{1},b_{1}\right),\ldots\left(a_{K},b_{K}\right)
. We also define the random variable
Z_{\text{test}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
so that

\left(Z_{\text{test}}\right)_{j}\equiv\llangle\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{\text{test}},\hat{\phi}_{j}\rrangle_{\hat{\mu}}.

(S269)

Note that the distribution of
Z_{\text{test}}
is not equal to that of
Z
, since
y_{\text{test}}
is independent of
\hat{\mu}
and the
\hat{\phi}_{j}
, while
y_{1}
is not.

We define the secondary intermediate scores as
U_{k}^{Z,1}\equiv a_{k}^{\top}Y_{\text{test}}
,
U_{k}^{Z,2}\equiv a_{k}^{\top}Z_{\text{test}}
, and
V_{k}^{Z}\equiv b_{k}^{\top}X_{\text{test}}
. The additional score for
U_{k}
arises due to the complexity of estimating
\hat{\mu}
and
\hat{\phi}_{j}
, whereas the proof for the bound on
V_{k}
is slightly simpler.

Following
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
Theorem 10.2.3, our goal is to show a probabilistic bound on
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(U_{k}-\hat{U}_{k}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(V_{k}-\hat{V}_{k}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]
as the sample size
N
, the number of principal components we select
d
, and the dimension of the high-dimensional data
p
go to infinite. We choose this notion of error, where we condition on the sample, because we can derive a result which is comparable to our results for the canonical vectors while also integrating out the randomness of
\left(X_{\text{test}},y_{\text{test}}\right)
.

We begin with the proof of the bound for
V_{k}
since it is slightly simpler than that of
U_{k}
. For convenience throughout the proofs, we drop the
k
in the notation of the scores and canonical vectors. For example, we write
U_{k},U_{k}^{Z,1},\eta_{k}
as
U,U^{Z,1},\eta
.

E.1
Proof of bound for high-dimensional score

The proof strategy is to decompose the quantity of interest
\left(V-\hat{V}\right)^{2}
into three parts:

\left(V-\hat{V}\right)^{2}\lesssim{\left(V-V^{(d)}\right)^{2}}+{\left(V^{(d)}-V^{Z}\right)^{2}}+{\left(V^{Z}-\hat{V}\right)^{2}}

(S270)

so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(V-\hat{V}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(V-V^{(d)}\right)^{2}}+{\left(V^{(d)}-V^{Z}\right)^{2}}+{\left(V^{Z}-\hat{V}\right)^{2}}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]

(S271)

\displaystyle\equiv\text{Term I }+\text{Term II }+\text{Term III}

(S272)

Term I:

\text{Term I}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(V-V^{(d)}\right)^{2}}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]={\left(V-V^{(d)}\right)^{2}}.

(S273)

since
V
and
V^{(d)}
are independent of the sample. Then, Theorem
B.3
gives the probabilistic bound

\text{Term I}=O_{P}\left(\frac{{\gamma_{1}^{*}}^{2}\lVert\mathscr{C}_{12}-\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}\lVert^{2}}{\min_{j\neq k}\left|{\gamma_{k}^{*}}^{2}-{\gamma_{j}^{*}}^{2}\right|}\right),

(S274)

since bounds in expectation imply probabilistic bounds. See Section
B.4
for definitions of the operator
\mathscr{C}_{12}
and its principal component approximation
\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}
.

Term II:

\text{Term II}=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(V^{(d)}-V^{Z}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right].

(S275)

Let the support
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots p\}
represent the indices of non-zero elements of
\theta
or
b
, with cardinality
|S|\leq 2s
. Denote
X_{\text{test},S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|S|}
as the random vector consisting of only the entries
\{\left(X_{\text{test}}\right)_{j}:j\in S\}
. Similarly, we define
\theta_{S}
and
b_{S}
from
\theta
and
b
respectively, as well as
\Sigma_{X_{\text{test}},S}
from
\Sigma_{X_{\text{test}}}
, following Section
D
.

By definition,

\displaystyle V^{(d)}-V^{Z}

\displaystyle=\theta^{\top}X_{\text{test}}-b^{\top}X_{\text{test}}

(S276)

\displaystyle=\theta_{S}^{\top}X_{\text{test},S}-b^{\top}_{S}X_{\text{test},S}

(S277)

\displaystyle=\left(\theta_{S}-b_{S}\right)^{\top}X_{\text{test},S}.

(S278)

Therefore,

Term II

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\theta_{S}-b_{S}\right)^{\top}X_{\text{test},S}X_{\text{test},S}^{\top}\left(\theta_{S}-b_{S}\right)\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]

(S279)

\displaystyle=\left(\theta_{S}-b_{S}\right)^{\top}\Sigma_{X,S}\left(\theta_{S}-b_{S}\right)

(S280)

\displaystyle=\left\|\Sigma_{X,S}^{1/2}\left(\theta_{S}-b_{S}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(S281)

We can bound this by appealing to equation (
S195
) and Lemma
D.15
, so that we can remove the factor of
\left\|\Sigma_{X,S}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}^{2}
. We obtain

\text{Term II}=O_{P}\left(\frac{s\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{3}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]^{2}}{{\operatorname{min}\left(\gamma_{k-1}-\gamma_{k},\gamma_{k}-\gamma_{k+1}\right)^{2}}}\frac{\tau d}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{4}\right]^{3/2}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}\right).

(S282)

Term III:

\text{Term III}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(V^{Z}-\hat{V}\right)^{2}}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right].

(S283)

By definition,

\displaystyle V^{Z}-\hat{V}

\displaystyle=b^{\top}X_{\text{test}}-\hat{\theta}^{\top}X_{\text{test}}

(S284)

\displaystyle=\left(b-\hat{\theta}\right)^{\top}X_{\text{test}}.

(S285)

Similarly to Term II, we obtain

\text{Term III}=\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\left(\hat{\theta}-b\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(S286)

We can appeal to equation (
S151
) following the same argument used to bound
\theta-\hat{\theta}
in the proof of Theorem
C.3
, but where we place a
\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}
in front of the relevant terms throughout to obtain

\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\left(\hat{\theta}-b\right)\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\theta\right\|_{2}}{\tilde{\gamma}^{2}}+\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\tilde{B}\right\|_{2}}{\tilde{\gamma}{\operatorname{min}\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{k-1}^{2}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2},\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k+1}^{2}\right)}}\right)\left\|\tilde{B}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}\tilde{B}-\hat{B}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\left(\tilde{B}-\hat{B}\right)\right\|_{2}}{\tilde{\gamma}}.

(S287)

Here,
\tilde{B}=\Sigma_{X}^{-1}\Sigma_{XZ}\Sigma_{Z}^{-1/2}
. Noting that

\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\tilde{B}=\Sigma_{X}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{XZ}\Sigma_{Z}^{-1/2}

(S288)

so
\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\tilde{B}\right\|_{2}=\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
and
\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\theta\right\|_{2}=1
, we have

\text{Term III}\lesssim\frac{1}{{\operatorname{min}\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{k-1}^{2}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2},\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k+1}^{2}\right)^{2}}}\left\|\tilde{B}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}\tilde{B}-\hat{B}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}\right\|_{2}^{2}+{\tilde{\gamma}^{-2}}\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\left(\tilde{B}-\hat{B}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(S289)

Now we bound the two relevant terms on the right-hand-side. By equation (
S85
), Lemma
C.8
, Remark
8
, and Lemma
C.7
, we have

\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}(\hat{B}-\tilde{B})\right\|_{F}^{2}=O_{P}\left(\kappa_{X}s\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}\frac{d\log p}{N}\right).

(S290)

By Corollary
C.1
,

\left\|\tilde{B}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}\tilde{B}-\hat{B}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}_{X}\hat{B}\right\|_{2}^{2}=O_{P}\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}s\kappa\frac{d\log p}{N}\right).

(S291)

Putting the last three equations together,

Term III

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}s\kappa}{{\operatorname{min}\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{k-1}^{2}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2},\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k+1}^{2}\right)^{2}}}\frac{d\log p}{N}+\tilde{\gamma}^{-2}\kappa_{X}s\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}\frac{d\log p}{N}\right)

(S292)

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}s\kappa}{{\operatorname{min}\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{k-1}^{2}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2},\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k+1}^{2}\right)^{2}}}\frac{d\log p}{N}\right).

(S293)

Final bound for

V_{k}
:

Now we can combine our bounds for terms I, II, and III to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(V-\hat{V}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{{\gamma_{1}^{*}}^{2}\lVert\mathscr{C}_{12}-\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}\lVert^{2}}{\min_{j\neq k}\left|{\gamma_{k}^{*}}^{2}-{\gamma_{j}^{*}}^{2}\right|}\right)

(S294)

\displaystyle+O_{P}\left(\frac{s\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{3}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]^{2}}{{\operatorname{min}\left(\gamma_{k-1}-\gamma_{k},\gamma_{k}-\gamma_{k+1}\right)^{2}}}\frac{\tau d}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{4}\right]^{3/2}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}\right)

(S295)

\displaystyle+O_{P}\left(\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}s\kappa}{{\operatorname{min}\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{k-1}^{2}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2},\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k+1}^{2}\right)^{2}}}\frac{d\log p}{N}\right).

(S296)

Simplifying this, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(V_{k}-\hat{V}_{k}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{{\gamma_{1}^{*}}^{2}\lVert\mathscr{C}_{12}-\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}\lVert^{2}}{\min_{j\neq k}\left|{\gamma_{k}^{*}}^{2}-{\gamma_{j}^{*}}^{2}\right|}\right)

(S297)

\displaystyle+O_{P}\left(\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}\tau s\kappa\omega_{d}^{-3}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{4}\right]^{3/2}}{\min_{j\neq k}\min\left\{\left|{\gamma_{k}}^{2}-{\gamma_{j}}^{2}\right|,\left|{\gamma_{k}}-{\gamma_{j}}\right|\right\}^{2}}\frac{d\log p}{N}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}\right).

(S298)

E.2
Proof of bound for Riemannian-functional score

The proof strategy is to decompose the quantity of interest
\left(U-\hat{U}\right)^{2}
into four parts:

\left(U-\hat{U}\right)^{2}\lesssim{\left(U-U^{(d)}\right)^{2}}+{\left(U^{(d)}-U^{Z,1}\right)^{2}}+{\left(U^{Z,1}-U^{Z,2}\right)^{2}}+{\left(U^{Z,2}-\hat{U}\right)^{2}}

(S299)

so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(U-\hat{U}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(U-U^{(d)}\right)^{2}}+{\left(U^{(d)}-U^{Z,1}\right)^{2}}+{\left(U^{Z,1}-U^{Z,2}\right)^{2}}+{\left(U^{Z,2}-\hat{U}\right)^{2}}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]

(S300)

\displaystyle\equiv\text{Term I }+\text{Term II }+\text{Term III}+\text{Term IV}

(S301)

Term I:

Following the same argument used in the proof for the bound on
V
, Theorem
B.3
gives the probabilistic bound

\text{Term I}=O_{P}\left(\frac{{\gamma_{1}^{*}}^{2}\lVert\mathscr{C}_{12}-\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}\lVert^{2}}{\min_{j\neq k}\left|{\gamma_{k}^{*}}^{2}-{\gamma_{j}^{*}}^{2}\right|}\right).

(S302)

Term II:

\text{Term II}=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(U^{(d)}-U^{Z,1}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right].

(S303)

By definition,

\displaystyle U^{(d)}-U^{Z}

\displaystyle=\eta^{\top}Y_{\text{test}}-a^{\top}Y_{\text{test}}

(S304)

\displaystyle=\left(\eta-a\right)^{\top}Y_{\text{test}},

(S305)

so that

Term II

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\eta-a\right)^{\top}Y_{\text{test}}Y_{\text{test}}^{\top}\left(\eta-a\right)\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]

(S306)

\displaystyle=\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}\left(\eta-a\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(S307)

\displaystyle=\left\|\tilde{\eta}-\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}a\right\|_{2}^{2}

(S308)

\displaystyle=\left\|\tilde{\eta}-\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}\Sigma_{Z}^{-1/2}\tilde{a}\right\|_{2}^{2}

(S309)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\tilde{\eta}-\tilde{a}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|\left(I-\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}\Sigma_{Z}^{-1/2}\right)\tilde{a}\right\|_{2}^{2}

(S310)

The first term on the right hand side shares the same bound as Term II in the proof for the bound for
V
, using Lemma
D.15
and equation (
S170
):

\left\|\tilde{\eta}-\tilde{a}\right\|_{2}^{2}=O_{P}\left(\frac{s\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{3}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]^{2}}{{\operatorname{min}\left(\gamma_{k-1}-\gamma_{k},\gamma_{k}-\gamma_{k+1}\right)^{2}}}\frac{\tau d}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{4}\right]^{3/2}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}\right).

(S311)

To bound
\left\|\left(I-\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}\Sigma_{Z}^{-1/2}\right)\tilde{a}\right\|_{2}^{2}
, we use
\left\|\tilde{a}\right\|_{2}=1
, Lemma
D.3
, and

\displaystyle\left\|\left(I-\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}\Sigma_{Z}^{-1/2}\right)\tilde{a}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}\left(\Sigma_{Y}^{-1/2}-\Sigma_{Z}^{-1/2}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}

(S312)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z-Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{3}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right].

(S313)

By Lemma
D.11
, we then have

\left\|\left(I-\Sigma_{Y}^{1/2}\Sigma_{Z}^{-1/2}\right)\tilde{a}\right\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{3}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\frac{\tau d}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{4}\right]^{3/2}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}.

(S314)

Combining these bounds and simplifying, we finally obtain

Term II

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{\omega_{1}\omega_{d}^{-3}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}\right)^{2}}{{\operatorname{min}\left(\gamma_{k-1}-\gamma_{k},\gamma_{k}-\gamma_{k+1}\right)^{2}}}\frac{\tau sd}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{4}\right]^{3/2}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}\right).

(S315)

Term III:

\text{Term III}=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(U^{Z,1}-U^{Z,2}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right].

(S316)

By definition,

\displaystyle U^{Z,1}-U^{Z,2}

\displaystyle=a^{\top}Y_{\text{test}}-a^{\top}Z_{\text{test}}

(S317)

\displaystyle=a^{\top}\left(Y_{\text{test}}-Z_{\text{test}}\right),

(S318)

so that by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Lemma
D.12
,

\displaystyle\left(U^{Z,1}-U^{Z,2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|a\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|Y_{\text{test}}-Z_{\text{test}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

(S319)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|Y_{\text{test}}-Z_{\text{test}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

(S320)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\left\|Y_{\text{test}}-Z_{\text{test}}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(S321)

Despite the fact that here we have
Z_{\text{test}}
, we can appeal to Lemma
D.11
to bound
\left\|Y_{\text{test}}-Z_{\text{test}}\right\|_{2}^{2}
, since replacing
y_{1}
with
y_{\text{test}}
does not change the proof. Using Lemma
D.11
and the law of total expectation we obtain

Term III

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y_{\text{test}}-Z_{\text{test}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]

(S322)

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\omega_{d}^{-1}\frac{\tau d}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{4}\right]^{3/2}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}\right).

(S323)

Term IV:

\text{Term IV}=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(U^{Z,2}-\hat{U}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right].

(S324)

By definition,

\displaystyle U^{Z,2}-\hat{U}

\displaystyle=a^{\top}Z_{\text{test}}-\hat{\eta}^{\top}Z_{\text{test}}

(S325)

\displaystyle=\left(a-\hat{\eta}\right)^{\top}Z_{\text{test}},

(S326)

so that similarly to Term III we obtain

\text{Term IV}\lesssim\left\|a-\hat{\eta}\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z_{\text{test}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right].

(S327)

While
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z_{\text{test}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\neq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]
, we can use the same argument we used to show
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]
to show that
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z_{\text{test}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}
(Lemma
D.12
). Then by the law of total expectation and Theorem
C.3
applied to
Z
and
X
, we have

Term IV

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{d\log(p)}{N}\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\operatorname{max}\left\{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}s\kappa}{{\operatorname{min}\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{k-1}^{2}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2},\tilde{\gamma}_{k}^{2}-\tilde{\gamma}_{k+1}^{2}\right)^{2}}},\left\|\Sigma_{Z}\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma_{Z}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\right\}\right)\cdot O_{P}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}\right)

(S328)

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{d\log(p)}{N}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}\right)\omega_{1}\omega_{d}^{-2}\frac{\gamma_{1}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}s\kappa}{{\operatorname{min}\left(\gamma_{k-1}^{2}-\gamma_{k}^{2},\gamma_{k}^{2}-\gamma_{k+1}^{2}\right)^{2}}}\right).

(S329)

Final bound for

U_{k}
:

Now we can combine our bounds for terms I, II, III and IV to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(U-\hat{U}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{{\gamma_{1}^{*}}^{2}\lVert\mathscr{C}_{12}-\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}\lVert^{2}}{\min_{j\neq k}\left|{\gamma_{k}^{*}}^{2}-{\gamma_{j}^{*}}^{2}\right|}\right)

(S330)

\displaystyle+O_{P}\left(\frac{\omega_{1}\omega_{d}^{-3}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}\right)^{2}}{{\operatorname{min}\left(\gamma_{k-1}-\gamma_{k},\gamma_{k}-\gamma_{k+1}\right)^{2}}}\frac{\tau sd}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{4}\right]^{3/2}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}\right)

(S331)

\displaystyle+O_{P}\left(\omega_{d}^{-1}\frac{\tau d}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{4}\right]^{3/2}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}\right)

(S332)

\displaystyle+O_{P}\left(\frac{d\log(p)}{N}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}\right)\omega_{1}\omega_{d}^{-2}\frac{\gamma_{1}^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}s\kappa}{{\operatorname{min}\left(\gamma_{k-1}^{2}-\gamma_{k}^{2},\gamma_{k}^{2}-\gamma_{k+1}^{2}\right)^{2}}}\right)

(S333)

Simplifying this gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(U_{k}-\hat{U}_{k}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{{\gamma_{1}^{*}}^{2}\lVert\mathscr{C}_{12}-\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}\lVert^{2}}{\min_{j\neq k}\left|{\gamma_{k}^{*}}^{2}-{\gamma_{j}^{*}}^{2}\right|}\right)

(S334)

\displaystyle+O_{P}\left(\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}\tau s\kappa\omega_{1}\omega_{d}^{-3}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{4}\right]^{3/2}}{\min_{j\neq k}\min\left\{\left|{\gamma_{k}}^{2}-{\gamma_{j}}^{2}\right|,\left|{\gamma_{k}}-{\gamma_{j}}\right|\right\}^{2}}\right)

(S335)

\displaystyle\cdot O_{P}\left(\frac{d\log p}{N}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{max}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}\right).

(S336)

We note that the second term on the right-hand side is nearly identical to the bound on
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(V-\hat{V}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]
, except here we have an additional
\omega_{1}
term. Therefore, we may concisely write the final bound on the score errors for both
U
and
V
together using the bound for
U
, since we have already assumed for convenience that
\omega_{1}\geq 1
.

Before stating the final bounds, we state a final lemma which allows us to use the infinite-dimensional canonical correlations.

Lemma E.1
.

For every
k=1,2,\ldots K
,
\gamma_{k}^{*}
is asymptotically equivalent to
\gamma_{k}
as
d
goes to infinite.

The proof follows directly from Theorem 4.2.8 of
Hsing and Eubank (2015)
applied to
\mathscr{C}_{12}\mathscr{C}_{21}
and
\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}\mathscr{C}_{21}^{(d)}
which gives that

\sup_{k\geq 1}\left|{\gamma_{k}^{*}}^{2}-\gamma_{k}^{2}\right|\leq\lVert\mathscr{C}_{12}\mathscr{C}_{21}-\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}\mathscr{C}_{21}^{(d)}\lVert.

(S337)

For bounded operators
A
and
B
with adjoints
A^{*}
and
B^{*}
, we have

AA^{*}-BB^{*}=(A-B)A^{*}-B(A^{*}-B^{*}),

(S338)

so that

\lVert AA^{*}-BB^{*}\lVert\leq\left(\lVert A\lVert+\lVert B\lVert\right)\lVert A-B\lVert.

(S339)

Applying this inequality with
A=\mathscr{C}_{12}
and
B=\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}
, the quantity on the right-hand side of equation (
S337
) converges to
0
as
d
grows to infinity since

\lVert\mathscr{C}_{12}-\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}\lVert\leq\lVert\mathscr{C}_{12}-\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}\lVert_{\text{HS}},

(S340)

which decays to
0
as
d
grows since
\mathscr{C}_{12}
being finite-dimensional means is it necessarily Hilbert-Schmidt.

We finally state the error bound on the convergence of the estimated canonical variables to the population infinite-dimensional canonical variables.

Theorem E.1

(Convergence of canonical variables)
.

Let
(U_{k},V_{k})
be the solution pair to the problem in Theorem
B.1
between
\chi_{1}=\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{\text{test}}
and
\chi_{2}=X_{\text{test}}
, for
k=1,\ldots K
. Let
\left(\hat{U}_{k},\hat{V}_{k}\right)\equiv\left(\llangle\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{\text{test}},\hat{\psi}_{k}\rrangle_{\hat{\mu}},\langle X_{\text{test}},\hat{\theta}_{k}\rangle\right)
, where
\hat{\theta}_{k}
,
\hat{\eta}_{k}
and
\hat{\mu}
have been estimated via Algorithm
1
.
Then, under Assumptions
D.1
-
D.4
, but without assuming a finite-dimensional correlation structure on
X
and
y
in Assumption
D.3
, we have

\displaystyle\max\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(U_{k}-\hat{U}_{k}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right],\mathbb{E}\left[\left(V_{k}-\hat{V}_{k}\right)^{2}\left.\right|\left(X_{i},y_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{N}\right]\right\}

(S341)

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{{\gamma_{1}^{*}}^{2}\lVert\mathscr{C}_{12}-\mathscr{C}_{12}^{(d)}\lVert^{2}}{\min_{j\neq k}\left|{\gamma_{k}^{*}}^{2}-{\gamma_{j}^{*}}^{2}\right|}+\frac{ds\log p}{N}\frac{\tau\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|_{2,\infty}\kappa\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y_{1}\right\|_{\mu}^{4}\right]^{3/2}}{\min_{j\neq k}\min\left\{\left|{\gamma_{k}^{*}}^{2}-{\gamma_{j}^{*}}^{2}\right|,\left|{\gamma_{k}^{*}}-{\gamma_{j}^{*}}\right|\right\}^{2}}\frac{\omega_{1}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}\right)^{2}}{\omega_{d}^{3}\underset{j=1,\ldots d}{\operatorname{min}}\left({\omega_{j}-\omega_{j+1}}\right)^{2}}\right).

(S342)

Appendix F
Additional identities and inequalities

In the proofs, we use several identities and inequalities involving matrices. For definitions of the various matrix operations used below we refer to Section
C.1
. In the following,
A
and
B
denote matrices for which the specified matrix multiplications are valid.

1.

For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,
\left\|x\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|x\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|x\right\|_{1}
.

2.

\left\|x\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{p}\left\|x\right\|_{\infty}
,
\left\|x\right\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{p}\left\|x\right\|_{2}
, and
\left\|x\right\|_{1}\leq p\left\|x\right\|_{\infty}
.

3.

\left\|A\right\|_{\operatorname{max}}\leq\left\|A\right\|_{2,\infty}\leq\left\|A\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|A\right\|_{F}\leq\left\|A\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}

4.

For induced norms,
\lVert AB\lVert_{\beta,\alpha}\leq\lVert A\lVert_{\gamma,\alpha}\lVert B\lVert_{\beta,\gamma}

(
Trefethen and Bau (2022)
equation (3.14)). In particular,
\lVert AB\lVert_{2}=\lVert AB\lVert_{2,2}\leq\lVert A\lVert_{\infty,2}\lVert B\lVert_{2,\infty}
.

5.

For any matrix
A
,
\left\|A\right\|_{2}=\left\|A^{\top}A\right\|_{2}^{1/2}
.

6.

In addition to its definition as the largest singular value,
\left\|A\right\|_{2}
is the norm induced by
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
and
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
.

7.

In addition to its definition as the norm induced by the
\lVert\cdot\lVert_{2}
and
\lVert\cdot\lVert_{\infty}
norms,
\left\|A\right\|_{2,\infty}=\operatorname{max}_{i}(\left\|A_{i}\right\|_{2})
, where
A_{i}
is the
i
th row of
A
. (
Cape et al. (2019)
Proposition 6.1.)

8.

\left\|AB\right\|_{2,\infty}\leq\left\|A\right\|_{2,\infty}\left\|B\right\|_{2}
. (
Cape et al. (2019)
Proposition 6.5.)

9.

For
B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times d}
,
\left\|B\right\|_{2,\infty}\leq\sqrt{d}\left\|B\right\|_{\operatorname{max}}
.

10.

For the induced norm
\lVert\cdot\lVert_{\infty,2}
,
\lVert B^{\top}\lVert_{\infty,2}\leq\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
.

11.

If
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times p}
, and
B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times d}
, then
\left\|AB\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|A^{\top}\right\|_{2,\infty}\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
. Additionally,
\left\|AB\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|A^{\top}\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\left\|B\right\|_{2,\infty}
.

12.

\left\|AB\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\leq\left\|A\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\left\|B\right\|_{2}
.

13.

If
A,B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
are positive definite, then
\left\|A^{1/2}-B^{1/2}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{max}\left(\left\|A^{-1}\right\|_{2},\left\|B^{-1}\right\|_{2}\right)^{1/2}\left\|A-B\right\|_{2}
.

14.

If
A,B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
are positive definite, then
\left\|A^{-1/2}-B^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{max}\left(\left\|A^{-1}\right\|_{2},\left\|B^{-1}\right\|_{2}\right)^{3/2}\left\|A-B\right\|_{2}
.

15.

For any positive definite matrices
A,B
of the same size, the product
AB
is diagonalizable with positive eigenvalues. Additionally,
AB
has the same eigenvalues as
(AB^{2}A)^{1/2}
. In particular,
\left\|AB-I\right\|_{2}=\left\|(AB^{2}A)^{1/2}-I\right\|_{2}
. Note that
AB
may not be symmetric, and therefore is not necessarily positive definite.

16.

For positive real numbers
x
and
y
,
\left|x^{-1}-y^{-1}\right|\leq{\operatorname{min}(x,y)}^{-3}\left|x^{2}-y^{2}\right|
.

17.

For
A,B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
,
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
\left\|Aa-Bb\right\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\left\|A-B\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|a\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|B\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|a-b\right\|_{2}^{2}

18.

For any norm
\lVert\cdot\lVert
,
\lVert a+b\lVert^{2}\leq 3\left(\lVert a\lVert^{2}+\lVert b\lVert^{2}\right)
.

19.

For matrices
A,B
of the same dimensions,
\left\|\left|A\right|-\left|B\right|\right\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{2}\left\|A-B\right\|_{F}
, where
\left|A\right|\equiv\left(A^{\top}A\right)^{1/2}
. (
Bhatia (2013)
equation VII.39)

Proof.

We prove the non-standard or non-straightforward results.

Proof of
10
:

To show
\lVert B^{\top}\lVert_{\infty,2}\leq\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
, we use the definition:

\lVert B^{\top}\lVert_{\infty,2}\equiv\underset{\left\|x\right\|_{\infty}=1}{\operatorname{sup}\ }\left\|B^{\top}x\right\|_{2}.

(S343)

Without loss of generality, let
B
be an element of
{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times d}
.

\left\|B^{\top}x\right\|_{2}=\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{p}b_{i}x_{i}\right\|_{2}
where
b_{i}
is the
i
th row of
B
, and
x_{i}
is the
i
th entry of
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
. For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
with
\left\|x\right\|_{\infty}=1
, we have

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{p}b_{i}x_{i}\right\|_{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{p}|x_{i}|\left\|b_{i}\right\|_{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{p}\left\|b_{i}\right\|_{2}=\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}},

(S344)

using the triangle inequality and because
\left\|x\right\|_{\infty}=1
. This completes the proof.

Proof of
11
:

To show
\left\|AB\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|A^{\top}\right\|_{2,\infty}\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
, we begin by using item
4
to obtain

\left\|AB\right\|_{2}=\left\|B^{\top}A^{\top}\right\|_{2}\leq\lVert B^{\top}\lVert_{\infty,2}\lVert A^{\top}\lVert_{2,\infty}.

(S345)

Using item
10
we have

\lVert B^{\top}\lVert_{\infty,2}\lVert A^{\top}\lVert_{2,\infty}\leq\left\|B\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\lVert A^{\top}\lVert_{2,\infty},

(S346)

completing the proof of the first statement. To show the second statement, we proceed similarly but apply item
4
to
\left\|AB\right\|_{2}
instead of
\left\|B^{\top}A^{\top}\right\|_{2}
.

Proof of
12
:

To show
\left\|AB\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\leq\left\|A\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\left\|B\right\|_{2}
, we begin with the definition. Without loss of generality,
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times q}
and
B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times r}
. We have

\left\|AB\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\left\|\left(AB\right)_{i}\right\|_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\left\|A_{i}^{\top}B\right\|_{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{p}\left\|a_{i}\right\|_{2}\left\|B\right\|_{2}=\left\|A\right\|_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\left\|B\right\|_{2},

(S347)

where
\left(C\right)_{i}
denotes the
i
th row of a matrix
C
and we have used item
6
.

Proof of
13
:

The statement follows from equation X.46 of
Bhatia (2013)
by choosing
r=1/2
and since the
2
-norm is unitarily invariant.

Proof of
14
:

We begin with the equality
A^{-1}-B^{-1}=A^{-1}\left(B-A\right)B^{-1}
, which holds for any square invertible matrices
A
and
B
of the same size. This implies

\left\|A^{-1}-B^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|A^{-1}\right\|_{2}\left\|A-B\right\|_{2}\left\|B^{-1}\right\|_{2}.

(S348)

We apply this to the matrices
A^{1/2}
and
B^{1/2}
to obtain

\left\|A^{-1/2}-B^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|A^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\left\|A^{1/2}-B^{1/2}\right\|_{2}\left\|B^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}.

(S349)

Using item
13
on
\left\|A^{1/2}-B^{1/2}\right\|_{2}
in the last inequality, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|A^{-1/2}-B^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{max}\left(\left\|A^{-1}\right\|_{2},\left\|B^{-1}\right\|_{2}\right)^{1/2}\left\|A-B\right\|_{2}\left\|A^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\left\|B^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}

(S350)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\|A^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\left\|B^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\operatorname{max}\left(\left\|A^{-1/2}\right\|_{2},\left\|B^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\right)\left\|A-B\right\|_{2}

(S351)

One of
\left\|A^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}
and
\left\|B^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}
is larger, and in either case, the statement to be proven holds.

Proof of
15
:

The first claim, that
AB
is diagonalizable with positive eigenvalues, follows from Proposition 6.1 of
Serre (2010)
. To show that
AB
has the same eigenvalues as
(AB^{2}A)^{1/2}
, let
C=AB
, so that
(AB^{2}A)^{1/2}=\left(CC^{\top}\right)^{1/2}
. Letting
U\Sigma V^{\top}=C
be a singular value decomposition of
C
, we have

\left(CC^{\top}\right)^{1/2}=\left(U\Sigma V^{\top}V\Sigma U^{\top}\right)^{1/2}=\left(U\Sigma^{2}U^{\top}\right)^{1/2}=U\Sigma U^{\top},

(S352)

where in the last line we have used that
C
has positive singular values from the first claim.

The last claim follows from the previous claim, since for a diagonalizable matrix
C
where
C=WDW^{-1}
with
D
diagonal, we have

C-I=WDW^{-1}-WIW^{-1}=W(D-I)W^{-1},

(S353)

so that
C-I
has eigenvalues equal to those of
C
minus
1
.

Proof of
16
:

Letting
f(x)=x^{-1/2}
, the mean value theorem implies

\left|f(x)-f(y)\right|\leq\operatorname{min}\left(x,y\right)^{-3/2}\left|x-y\right|.

(S354)

Plugging in
x^{2}
for
x
and
y^{2}
for
y
, we obtain the result.

Proof of
17
:

The statement follows from adding and subtracting
Ba
and the inequality
\left\|a+b\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\left\|a\right\|_{2}^{2}+2\left\|b\right\|_{2}^{2}
.

Proof of
18
:

From the triangle inequality, we have

\left\|a+b\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\left(\left\|a\right\|_{2}+\left\|b\right\|_{2}\right)^{2}=\left\|a\right\|_{2}^{2}+2\left\|a\right\|_{2}\left\|b\right\|_{2}+\left\|b\right\|_{2}^{2}

(S355)

One of
\left\|a\right\|_{2}
or
\left\|b\right\|_{2}
is larger than the other, and in either case, we deduce that
\left\|a\right\|_{2}^{2}+2\left\|a\right\|_{2}\left\|b\right\|_{2}+\left\|b\right\|_{2}\leq 3\left(\left\|a\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|b\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)
, completing the proof.
∎

Appendix G
Intrinsic RFPCA algorithm

For completeness, in this section, we outline the main steps of the Intrinsic RFPCA algorithm. Let
M
be the dimension of
\mathcal{M}
,
d
the number of principal components to compute,
N
the number of observations, and
L
the number of time-steps.

1.

Estimate
\mu:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{M}
, the functional Frechét mean of
y
, by computing the Frechét mean of
\{y_{i}(t_{l})\}_{i}
separately for each point in
\{t_{l}\}_{l=1,\ldots,L}
where the functional data are observed.

2.

Compute the linear representations
\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{i}\in L^{2}(T\hat{\mu})
, for
i=1,\ldots N
. In practice, this can be done by computing
\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}(t_{l})}y_{i}(t_{l})\in T_{\hat{\mu}(t_{l})}\mathcal{M}
for every
t_{l}
by using the
\operatorname{Log}
map on
\mathcal{M}
.

3.

Let
E(x)
be an orthonormal frame for the tangent space centered at
x\in\mathcal{M}
(see Section
G.1
for an example). An orthonormal frame is a collection of tangent bases
E(x)=\{E_{1}(x),\ldots E_{M}(x)\}
for
T_{x}\mathcal{M}
, with
x\in\mathcal{M}
, which varies smoothly with
x
, i.e. for each
k=1,\ldots M
,
E_{k}(x)
is a smooth map from
\mathcal{M}
to
T\mathcal{M}
. For any fixed
x\in\mathcal{M}
, the functions
\{E_{k}(x)\}
are orthonormal with respect to the inner product on
T_{x}\mathcal{M}
, that is,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{x}
.

Compute the functional ‘coefficients’
\hat{Z}_{i}:\mathcal{T}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
of the expansion of
\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}}y_{i}:\mathcal{T}\to T\mathcal{M}
relative to
E
, for each
i=1,\ldots N
. In practice,
\hat{Z}_{i}(t_{l})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
is computed separately for every
l=1,\ldots L
. The
k
th entry of
\hat{Z}_{i}(t_{l})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
is computed as
\langle\operatorname{Log}_{\hat{\mu}(t_{l})}y_{i}(t_{l}),E_{k}(\hat{\mu}(t_{l})\rangle_{\hat{\mu}(t_{l})}
for
k=1,\ldots M
. The resulting
\{\hat{Z}_{i}\}
are estimates of the realizations of a real vector-valued random process
Z:\mathcal{T}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
, with
k
th component
Z_{k}:\mathcal{T}\to{\mathbb{R}}
given by
Z_{k}(t)=\langle\operatorname{Log}_{\mu(t)}y(t),E_{k}(\mu(t)\rangle_{\mu(t)}
.

The process
Z
is a real vector-valued process with the same principal scores as the process
\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y
, and the
j
th principal component of
Z
,
\pi_{j}:\mathcal{T}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
, is related to the
j
th principal component of
\operatorname{Log}_{\mu}y
,
\phi_{j}\in L^{2}(T\mu)
, via
\phi_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\pi_{jk}E_{k}
. Here,
\pi_{jk}
denotes the
k
th entry of
\pi_{j}
for
k=1,\ldots M
.

4.

Apply Multivariate Functional Principal Component Analysis (MFPCA)
(
Happ and Greven, 2018
)
to the functions
\{\hat{Z}_{i}\}
to estimate
d
principal component functions
\hat{\pi}_{j}
of the functional coefficients.

Estimate the principal component functions
\{\phi_{j}\}
of the
\operatorname{Log}
representations of the functional data as
\hat{\phi}_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\hat{\pi}_{jk}E_{k}(\hat{\mu})
, for
j=1,\ldots d
. Then, estimate the associated scores as
\hat{Y}_{ij}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}Z_{i}(t_{l})^{\top}\hat{\pi}_{j}(t_{l})
, for
j=1,\ldots d
and
i=1,\ldots N
. The score estimates do not depend on the choice of the orthonormal frame
E
(Proposition 5, item 2 of
Lin and Yao (2019)
). Compute variance estimates
\hat{\omega}_{j}=\operatorname{Var}(\hat{Y}_{ij})
.

5.

Return the estimated scores
\{\hat{Y}_{ij}\}
, variances
\{\hat{\omega}_{j}\}
and principal component functions
\{\hat{\phi}_{j}\}
.

G.1
An orthonormal frame for the manifold of SPD matrices

Here we provide an explicit construction of an orthonormal frame in the setting where
\mathcal{M}
is the manifold of
{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times m}
symmetric positive matrices equipped with the affine invariant metric, as in our application setting. The maps
\operatorname{Log}
and
\operatorname{Exp}
maps are defined as
\operatorname{Log}_{F}(G)=F^{1/2}\operatorname{log}\left(F^{-1/2}GF^{-1/2}\right)F^{1/2}
and
\operatorname{Exp}_{F}(W)=F^{1/2}\operatorname{exp}\left(F^{-1/2}WF^{-1/2}\right)F^{1/2}
. Moreover, the inner product at
F\in\mathcal{M}
between
W,Z\in T_{F}\mathcal{M}
is defined as
\langle W,Z\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}=\operatorname{tr}\left(F^{-1}WF^{-1}Z\right)
. In this setting, we can use the result from Section 3.3.3.3. of
Pennec et al. (2019)
, which provides an explicit construction of an orthonormal frame
E(F)
for the tangent bundle, evaluated at an arbitrary
F\in\mathcal{M}
. This can be defined as

E_{ij}\left(F\right)=\begin{cases}\left(F^{1/2}e_{i}\right)\left(F^{1/2}e_{i}\right)^{\top}&(1\leq i=j\leq m)\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\left(F^{1/2}e_{i}\right)\left(F^{1/2}e_{j}\right)^{\top}+\left(F^{1/2}e_{j}\right)\left(F^{1/2}e_{i}\right)^{\top}\right)&(1\leq i<j\leq m),\end{cases}

(S356)

where
e_{i}
denotes the
i
th standard unit vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
, and
F\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times m}
. For a fixed
F\in\mathcal{M}
, there are
M=m(m+1)/2
unique
E_{ij}\left(F\right)
, where
M
is the dimension of
\mathcal{M}
.
We also note that the iterative algorithm proposed in
Cheng et al. (2016)
can be used to estimate the Fréchet mean
\mu
, and is detailed in equations (13) and (14) of
Cheng et al. (2016)
.
